3015. JOSEPH SIMPSON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Oct., 1/2 lb. weight of tea, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of the London Docks Company.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM CORNWELL . I am gate-keeper at the London Docks. On the 14th of Oct. I stopped the prisoner, who was a labourer—he was leaving, between five and six o'clock in the evening—on rubbing him down behind felt something concealed—I asked what he had—he said, "Nothing"—I informed Dix.
GEORGE DIX . I am a constable of the docks. I searched the prisoner, and found in the seat of his trowsers two pockets, each containing four ounces of green tea.
WILLIAM KEELING . I am foreman in the tea department of the London Docks. The prisoner was working there under me—here is a sample of tea from the place where he was working, and the tea found upon him—this is the same tea, I am sure—he had no right to take tea—I retched his trowsers—I found the pockets that had had the tea in them had been torn out, but the stitches are left—the tea was not in paper.
Prisoner. Q. Can you swear to this tea being the Company's? A. It is similar to the tea there.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going to the water-closet, and found this tea in the gangway.
GUILTY . Aged 61.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3016]
3016. ALICE BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Oct., 1 pair of trowsers, value 6 s.; the goods of Edward Alexander and another.
THOMAS CALVER . I am shopman to Edward and Charles Alexander, pawnbrokers, Church-street, Marylebone. On the 7th of Oct., about six o'clock in the evening, there were some hats outside the shop—I saw them fall—I was, picking them up—some one spoke to me, and I stopped the prisoner three or four doors off—I pulled her cloak on one side, and found a pair of trowsers, which were safe not two minutes before.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRT. Q. Was there a little' boy you were talking to? A. Yes—I know the prisoner—she was smiling till I took her back—I do not recollect her saying anything to me—she came to our house about a declaration.
MARY ANN DUFFELL . I am the wife of Joseph Duffell, and live opposite the prosecutor's shop—I saw the prisoner looking at the trowsers—I saw some hats fall, and while Calver was picking them up, the prisoner put the trowsers under her cloak, picked up one of the hats, and gave it to Calver—she stood a short time, and then walked away—I told Calver—he followed her.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable.) On this evening the prisoner was given into my custody—she said she only put the trowsers under her cloak to see if the gentleman would miss them.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18431023-3017]
3017. ROBERT HUME was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of Oct., 1 pair of shoes, value 5 s.; the goods of William Middleton.
WILLIAM MIDDLETON . I am a labourer, and work for Mr. Tossey. On Saturday, the 21st of Oct., about half-past eleven o'clock, I left a pair of shoes on Mr. Tossey's premises—I saw them safe at two, and at five I missed them—the prisoner was there nearly all day—I asked if he knew where they were—he said "No"—these are them.
JOHN HARRISON ( police-constable.) The prosecutor brought the prisoner to the station—I got these shoes from Read.
CAROLINE READ . I keep a general shop in Narrow-street, Limehouse. Between six o'clock and half-past, on Saturday evening, the prisoner came to my place with a young man, considerably older and much taller than himself—he asked if I bought old clothes—I said "Yes"—the other one said he had a pair of old shoes to sell—I looked at them, aud said they did not suit me—he said he was going on a tramp, he had had no work for sixteen weeks—he gave his name, Wilks—I asked the prisoner, and he said his name was Thompson—I bought them for 1 s. 4 d.—I gave the money to the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I was with this lad; he told me he was going to steal the shoes; he called to me, pulled out the shoes, and said, "I have got them, and I know where to sell them."
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Ten Days.
[trialAccount t18431023-3018]
3018. JANE KENSLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Oct., 2 pairs of stockings, value 9 d.; 2 yards of ribbon, 6 d.; 1 yard of net, 3 d.; 1 shirt, 6 d.; 1 cap, 4 d.; 1 towel, 1 d.; and 1 printed book, 6 d.; the goods of James Hancock, her master.
ANN HANCOCK . I am the wife of James Hancock, who keeps the Exeter Arms, Lisson-grove—the prisoner was my servant for three weeks. On the 22nd of Oct. I missed a pair of my black silk stockings from a drawer in a spare room—on the morning of the 23rd I told the prisoner I had missed them and several other things, and I would have a general search—she ran down to the wash-house, and came up with her keys, unlocked her box, and in it I found this other pair of silk stockings, and these other things—I asked how she could be guilty of such meanness—she said some one must have put them in her box, that the ribbon was belonging to a bonnet, and she had had it two years; but it is mine, it is quite new, and had never been on a bonnet—I have the fellow piece to it—I found a book of mine in her pocket—these are all my husband's things.
Prisoner. Between five and ten o'clock in the morning my box was opened, and some of the things were shut between the lid and the box. witness. I do not believe it was open—she unlocked it herself—I never had the key in my hand.
GERTRUDE HAWKRIDGE . I was present when the prisoner opened her box—she said, before the box was opened, if there was anything in it, it was placed there by some person—she said this ribbon had been round her bonnet, she had had it two years—it is quite new, and has never been pinned on.
CORNELIUS LOVEGROVE ( policeman.) I took the prisoner—she said, "I am innocent; the things have been put into my box by some person."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3019]
3019. ESTHER DODGE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of oct., 91bs. weight of soap, value 3 s.; and 1 pencil-case, 2 d.; the goods of charles Hawkins, her master: and CHARLOTTE FLETCHER , for feloniously receiving 4lbs. of soap, part of the same goods, knowing them to be stolen; against the Statute, &c.; to which
DODGE pleaded GUILTY . Aged 50.— Confined Four Months.
CHARLES HAWKINS . I live in Chichester-place, Gray's-inn-road, Dodge was my charwoman—she did not sleep in my house—at seven o'clock in the morning of the 10th of Oct. I was awoke by an officer—Dodge was then in the house—I went down to the butter-cellar, and found the key in the door, which was unlocked—I had three or four hundred weight of soap there—this soap is very much like what I had there—I cannot swear to it, there is no mark on it—I did not miss any—I can swear to this pencil-case—I saw it last on the counter, some time the week before—I usually kept it in the drawer in the shop.
SAMUEL PADDY . I am shop-boy to the prosecutor. On the night of the 9th of Oct., I locked the cellar-door where the soap was kept, and put the key in my candlestick, when I went to bed, and left it on the kitchen drawers—I was of first in the morning—Dodge came about seven o'clock—she went and lit the fire in the kitchen—after that, Fletcher came to the door, and asked to see Dodge—she spoke to Fletcher outside the door—Dodge had nothing with her then—shortly after a constable came—I then went to my matter, and saw the key in the cellar-door—no one was up till he came, but Dodge and me.
GEORGE PORTSMOUTH ( policeman.) On this morning, about seven o'clock, I saw Fletcher at the prosecutor's door—I watched, and saw Dodge come out and give Fletcher a bag, about twenty yards from the door—Dodge went into the house two or three times, and Fletcher remained standing outside—she walked away, and I stopped her, about 200 yards from the house—I asked what she had got—she at first said she did not know, and then she said it was no use telling me a lie, she had got three cakes of soap, which Mrs. Dodge gave her to take home—I went to the prosecutor's, and there took Dodge—she took a pencil-case out of her pocket, said she had got a pencil-case of Mr. Hawkins's, and gave it him.
Fletcher. I did not know the soap was stolen. Dodge gave it me to take home to her house.
FLETCHER — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18431023-3020]
3020. CAROLINE MOORE was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of Sept., 5 yards of merino, value 15 s.; 1 shawl, 18 s.; 1 bhroket, 10 s.; 1 sheet, 12 s. 6 d.; 3 night-gowns, 5 s.; 1 fiat-iron, 6 d.; and 1 bolster, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Dennis Ryan; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
BRIDGET RYAN . I am the wife of Dennis Ryan, and live in Great Wild-street, Lincoln's-inn-fields. The prisoner lodged with me in the same room—she left about five minutes before twelve o'clock on Tuesday, in the middle of last month, without giving notice—half an hour after she was gone, I missed three night gowns out of my box, and the other articles stated—the prisoner was brought to me on the 14th of Oct. by another women—I asked what she did with nay things—she said she sold all the best of them, and had pawned the children's frocks—I asked if she had sold my black shawl and merino dress—she said, "Yes, Mrs. Ryan, I have; do what you like, and give me up to the law"—this is my child's frock and night-gown—this is my flat iron—I redeemed them.
JAMES WILSON . I am in the employ of James Sayer, pawnbroker, Drury-lane. This frock was pawned by a woman who gave the name of Ann Moss—I do not know whether it was the prisoner—this is the ticket I gave her.
THOMAS COGSWELL . I am in the employ of Mr. Newbon, pawnbroker,
in Drury-lane. This night-gown was pawned on the 12th of Sept in the name of Ann Moss.
ELIZABETH M'DONALD . I char for Mrs. Ryan. I was present when the prisoner came there with another woman—I heard Mrs. Ryan ask what she had done with the things—she said the best of the things she had sold, but did not know to whom—she had pledged the rest.
RICHARD POWELL . I went to Great Wild-street, and found the prisoner—the prosecutrix gave her into custody.
Prisoner's Defence. I heard of a friend being in town, and I unfortunately took these little things to raise a few shillings to get a gown out; it was not with an intention to take them from her, but to return them. I am sure, when I came away, I left a pair of sheets on my bed, and a pair on another bed where two men lodged.
JAMES M'GREGOR ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3021]
3021. ANN GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of Oct., 1 pewter pot, value 1 s., the goods of Alfred Poole.
ALFRED POOLE . I keep the Red Lion public-house, in Cowcross-street. This pewter pot is mine, and has my name on it—on the 9th of Oct. the prisoner was in my house—Finlayson gave me some information, I took the prisoner, and took the pot from her.
RICHARD FINLAYSON . I live at Cow-cross—I saw the prisoner take the pot off the vinegar-cask, and put it into her pocket—I told the officer.
WILLIAM MARTIN ( policeman.) I took the prisoner, and took this pot from her right-hand pocket—I asked her what she had got there—she said, "Nothing"—I said, "It is a pint pot"—she said she did not know how it came there.
Prisoner. I was very tipsy. Witness. She was not.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18431023-3022]
3022. THOMAS ASTON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of Sept., 2 gold-beater's moulds, value 12 l.; and 3 half-ounce weights of gold, 14 l.; the goods of James Wright and another, his masters.
MR. MELLOR conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES WRIGHT . I am a gold-beater, in partnership with William Wright, and live in Hackney-road. The prisoner was in my service for two years—between six and seven o'clock in the morning of the 10th of Sept. 1842, I gave him a box, containing two gold-beaters' moulds, and three half-ounces of gold, in a partly manufactured state—he worked down stairs in the shop, and I saw nothing more of him till he was taken—I went into an upper shop, and between seven and eight o'clock the bell rang for me—I went down, and his partner gave me information—I found he was gone—I then saw his box, and found it was empty—I sent to the police-office—I did not see the prisoner again till the 13th of Oct., thirteen mdntbs afterwards—I then saw him opposite Haggerston church—I collared him, and said, "Halloo, I have got you at last"—he said, "For God's sake, don't take my liberty from me, I will pay you"—I said, "We must investigate this matter further"—he afterwards begged of me to give him his liberty for the sake of his wife and children—I said, "What is the use of talking of your wife and children, when you have been of no use to them the last thirteen months? you have not been near them."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where bad the box been kept all night? A. In my bed-room—I gave it him with the cover on it, as I recived it the previous night, and in about an hour I found the prisoner was gone—when he worked for us he lived in Ironmonger-street, St. Luke's—I think we had at that time about ten persons in our employ.
JAMES HADDON . I live in Young's-building, Old-street. I worked for the prosecutor—on the morning of the 10th of Sept., 1842, I recollect entering the shop about seven o'clock—I saw the prisoner there at work—he continued his work for about twenty minutes—he then said he was unwell, and begged of me a few pence to get some spirits, and left the shop—after he left, Hancock, the apprentice, came in, and James Ball, and Mr. White, a fellow-workman—White opened the box to go to work, but he had no tools to work with—this is the box I saw opened by White—it is a box which is used for putting a man's tools in that he wants to work with, and his gold—it stood between the prisoner and White—the tools were put in at night to give the box to the lad, who gave it to his employer—White found the moulds and a quantity of gold missing—I had seen the prisoner working with the moulds and the gold the day before.
Cross-examined. Q. When you came in, was anybody there but the prisoner? A. No, not in that shop.
GEROGE FIREASH ( police-constable N 159.) I took the prisoner—he said nothing.
JAMES WRIGHT re-examined. Q. Is this the box you gave the prisoner that morning? A. I believe it to be so—there were perhaps eight boxes—the men's boxes for the time had their names on a piece of paper on the top—this is one of our boxes, but I could not swear to it.
COURT. Q. When you gave it him, did you look in and see whether the gold was in it? A. No; but had it been empty I should have discovered it—this box has remained in my custody ever since.
WILLIAM WRIGHT . On the 9th of Sept. the prisoner asked me for a piece of gold to beat into gold-leaf, and I gave it him.
JAMES BALE . I was in the workshop on the evening of the 9th of Sept.—I saw White put away the tools and gold in the box—it was after eight o'clock—I took that box with others, and delivered it to Mr. James Wright.
JAMES WRIGHT re-examined. I cannot safely swear that I recollect receiving the box from Bale—I had been very ill all the week—if I received them, they were placed in my bed-room, locked up, and given to the men in the morning.
Mr. Mellor. Q. It seems that this box was used after the prisoner left, and the name that was on it was not taken notice of? A. No it was not—there might be a dozen boxes in the shop made all alike.
JOSEPH WHITE . I was in the prosecutor's employ, working with the prisoner on the same work—on Friday, the 9th of Sept, I put the work and the tools in the box, and gave it to Bale—on the Saturday morning I went to work, about twenty minutes after seven o'clock—I found the particular mould I had to work with, in the prisoner's place—he had done some work from it—I then missed two moulds and some gold from the box—I should not know the box now.
GEORGE STEAD . I was in the prosecutor's employ—I went there about half-past six o'clock, on the morning of the 10th of Sept.—the prisoner let me in—I went and stood beside him—he was weighing his gold, to see what he had, and told me to light the fire in the back shop—I went, and when I came back he was gone.
GUILTY. Aged 41.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3023]
3023. ANN DAWSON was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of Oct., I bolster-case, value 2 s.; the goods of Samuel Perry.
SAMUEL PERRY . I keep an eating-house in King-street, Westminster. On the 9th of Oct, about ten o'clock at night, the prisoner took a bed for the night—next day, about half-past two, she came and asked for a bed for the following night—she was asked for the money—she felt her pocket, and said she had left her money with some goods at a public-house near the railway—she then went into the parlour, and I was told something—I went to the bed-room and missed the bolster-case—I said I would fetch a policeman—this bolster-case was found on the prisoner—she said she took it to keep her warm—it is mine.
JOHN BRANSON ( policeman.) I took the prisoner to the station—I produce this bolster-case.
LUCY DUKE . I was called to search the prisoner—I found this bolster-case under her dress—she said she put it there to keep herself warm.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Ten Days.

3026. HENRY BELL was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Oct, 1 tea-chest, value 1 s.; and 38lbs. weight of tea, 7 l.; the goods of William Golding.
ROBERT OVERTON . I am a porter, and live in New-road, Mile-end. On the 24th of Oct., about four o'clock in the afternoon, I was in Fenchurch-street—I saw Hosier go up Fenchurch-court, and leave his cart at the corner—I saw the prisoner get up in front, and take off a chest of tea—I followed him to Billiter-street—seeing no officer, I went and asked him where he was going—he made no answer, but threw the chest down, hit me on the shins
with it, and ran away—I ran, and kept him in sight—some gentlemen stopped him in a court leading from Billiter-square—I came up and collared him—I am sure he is the person—I never lost sight of him—I gave him to an officer—I had asked a man at the East India warehouse to take care of the chest of tea when the prisoner threw it down—I went to the warehouse for it, and they would not give it up—they sent an officer with me from the station to get it—this is it.
PETER HOSIER . I am in the service of Mr. William Golding. I stopped my cart at the corner of Fen-court—I went up the court—I returned in about four minutes, and missed the chest of tea—I ran, and saw the prisoner collared by Overton—this is the chest.
WILLIAM GOLDING . I am the master of Hosier. This chest of tea weighs 38lbs.—it is worth about 7 l.—it was given into my charge by Mr. Thomas Phillips, and was going to Great Ormond-street from the London Docks—I had the care of it as a carrier.
THOMAS PHILLIPS . I am a custom-house agent, and live in Forest-row, Dalston. I was instructed to pay the Dock charges on this chest of tea—I saw it deposited in Gold ing's cart, and Hosier had the care of it.
GEORGE JAMES GOODMAN ( City police-constable, No. 591.) The prisoner was given into my charge by Overton—I afterwards received this chest—the prisoner said he was employed by a party to take it out of the cart, and carry it to a cab- stand in Leadenhall-street.
Prisoner's Defence. I was in Fenchurch-street, a gentlemanly-looking man came and asked me to carry this box to a cab- stand; I asked him if this was the cab- stand; he said, "No, I have a cab waiting in Leadenhall-street;"
I was taking it there, was pursued, and threw it down.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Nine Months.

3027. EDWARD SWAINE was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of Sept., 1 sovereign, 1 crown, 1 shilling, and 1 sixpence, the monies of Elisabeth Benson.
ELIZABETH BENSON . I am a widow, and live at Limehonie. I take in clothes to wash—about four weeks ago, I went out at half past eleven—the prisoner was lodging with me—I left him in my kitchen, and nobody else—I returned in twenty minutes, and he was gone—about four o'clock in the afternoon I went to a box, in which I had some money; it was all gone—I had one sovereign, one crown, one shilling and a sixpence there—my box was not locked—I saw the prisoner in a beer-shop about three weeks after, and gave him in charge.
JAMES HOWARD ( policeman.) On the evening of the 18th of Oct., I was in Salmon's-lane, Limehouse—the prosecutrix spoke to me—I went to a beer-shop, and took the prisoner—he said he went about ten in the morning and took 1 s., and got something to eat; he returned and missed half-a-crown, and he took 6 s. 6 d. that remained, but he knew nothing about the sovereign.

On the evening of the 24th of Oct., the prisoner came for half a pint of ale—he went into the bar parlour—I saw him take this quart pot, and put it into his pocket—when he went out, I went and stopped him—I told him he had a pot—he said he had no such thing about him—I gave him into custody—the pot was found between my house and where I stopped him.

3029. WILLIAM WARDELL was indicted for stealing, on the 27th of Sept., 1 chest of drawers, value 2 l. 10 s.; and 1 mat, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Deal, his master: and EDWARD WARDELL , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to be stolen; against the Statute, &c., to which
WILLIAM WARDELL pleaded GUILTY .
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM WAINE . I am shopman to Mr. John Dent, an upholsterer, in Crawford-street, Marylebone. On the 27th of Sept. a chest of drawers were removed from Montague-street to our warehouse—I missed them in the evening—I dismissed William Wardell, who was in the service—on the day after I searched William's premises, and saw a chest of drawers—they were the same that had been taken from my master—the mat produced is also his, and worth 1 s. 6 d.
JAMES COULTHARD . I am in the prosecutor's service—on the 27th of Sept. I assisted William Wardell to remove a chest of drawers—I have seen them since.
MORRIS ELLICOTT . I am a shoemaker, and live in Highworth-street, Lisson-grove. William Wardell lodged at my house—on the 27th Sept. he brought home a chest of drawers—I assisted him to put them in the kitchen.
JOHN PHELPS ( police-constable D 13.) About five in the afternoon of the 29th of Sept, I went to the house of Edward Wardell, in Upper Boston-place, and saw a mat there—at nine the same evening I took him into custody for reoeiving stolen property—he made no answer—I said I had found a door-mat at his house—he said he had bought it of his brother for 4 s. 8 d.—I had not then found the chest of drawers—I went to Stafford-street, Lisson-grove, and found a chest of drawers—I have shown them to Waine.
RICHARD STANFORD . I am pot-boy at the Hope and Anchor, in Hereford-street, Lisson-grove. On Friday evening, the 29th, about eight o'clock, Edward Wardell came and asked me to assist him with a chest of drawers a few doors off—I went with him to No. 4, Highworth-street, and assisted him to take a pair of drawers to Stafford-street, and left them in the front parlour—he gave me 4 d. and some beer.
EDWARD WARDELL — NOT GUILTY .

3031. JOHN GREEN and WILLIAM WEAVER were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Oct., 1 basket, value 1 s.; and 1 bushel of damsons, 5 s.; the goods of Gabriel Hards, the master of Green.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD MOORE . I am beadle of Covent-garden market. On the afternoon of the 2nd of Oct. I was employed watching Green, who was servant to the prosecutor, who keeps a shop in the market—I saw Green empty some damsons out of a bushel basket, at the prosecutor's stand, into a smaller sieve which laid on the ground—Weaver was close by—Green left the basket for some minutes, between Weaver's brother's stand and the prosecutor's—Weaver was near him at the time, and could see what was done—I made some inquiries of the prosecutor's daughter, then went back to the stall, and the basket was gone.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. Weaver is a porter employed in the market? A. Yes.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Weaver's brother has a stand next to the prosecutor's? A. Yes—he was between the two stands—there were no damsons on Weaver's stand—I have not told any one that they must not come up to give the prisoners a character, nor said, that if any money was subscribed towards the prisoner's defence, those persons should never be employed in the market again—Green lives in Duke's-court, Bow-street.
JOSEPH BAXTER . I am a porter in Covent-garden market, and live in Great Wild-street. On the afternoon of the 2nd of Oct. I watched Green by desire of Moore—he was standing at his master's stand—he turned, out, some damsons, and put them down, partly in his master's stand and partly in Weaver's brother's stand—he then spoke to Weaver—they talked together two or three minutes—after that Green went back to his work—Weaver followed him, and took the same damsons on his head to Duke's-court—he took the basket off his head, and put it into the passage, inside the door of No. 14—he came out with the empty basket—I saw Green taken, and heard Johnson ask him if he had authorized Weaver to take any damsons—he said no—he asked if he had authorized any one else—he made no reply—Johnson asked Weaver what became of those damsons he had taken up Bow-street—he said he had taken them to Green's house, by Green's direction—(Green lives at No. 16, and Wearer lives at No. 14)—I then went to No. 16, Duke's court, with Weaver—I heard him ask Green's wife where those damsons were that he took there a little while ago for John—she denied all knowledge of them—I am positive Weaver took them to No. 14—I stood in the court till he came out with the empty basket.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. How long was Weaver in the house before he came out? A. About half an hour—it was a foreign basket—there is an enormous quantity of foreign fruit sold in the market—I was walking up and down, while I was waiting there—Weaver took the basket a little before three o'clock in the afternoon—there were a great many people about—Weaver's brother has a stand outside his shop, and the prosecutor has the same—Green was at his own work at his master's stand, not inside the shop—Green gave no directions to Weaver, to my knowledge—Weaver was at the end of Russel-street when Green went to speak to him in the first instance.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Do not you know that Weaver keeps a greengrocer's shop? A. Yes—I was not above three doors from him when he went into the house—when Green took the basket, Weaver's stood was shut—there were other baskets about there.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When you were in this court, were you in a situation to see whether Weaver came out of No. 14 to go to No. 16? A. Yes, I can positively say he never came out of No. 14 till he came out with the empty basket, and he then came out of the court—I have no doubt it was the same basket.
FREDERICK JOHN JOHNSON . I am a constable of Covent-garden market. On the 2nd of Oct. I saw Weaver in Bow-street, with a sieve on his head—I could not see whether it was full or empty—he was walking in the direction to Duke's-court—I saw Baxter, and made a communication to him—after he had watched he made a communication to me—I took Green into custody, and asked if he authorized any one to take any damsons from his master's stand—he said "No"—I asked if he were sure of it—he said "Yes"—I asked if he authorized Slaymaker to take any—he said "No"—I asked if he authorized any one else—he said "No"—I took him to the station—I took Weaver the same day, and asked where he took that sieve to, which I saw on his head—he said, "To Green's house"—I asked by whose authority—he said, "By Green's."
GABRIEL HARDS . I am a fruit-salesman, and have a stand in Covent-garden market—I live in Basing-place, Lambeth. Green was in my employ about four years—he never sold, or had authority to sell for me, without my daughter was present—I know Weaver—I had made no sale to him of any damsons on the 2nd of Oct., and he had no authority from me to remove any—when I left the market there were upwards of forty if not fifty baskets of damsons—some damsons were pointed out to me by the officer as takes by a man named Slaymaker—the others were not found.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. I believe Green was a tenant of yours? A. Yes, he occupied a room of mine in this house in Duke's-court—the other rooms are let out to a number of persons—I had no knowledge of where Weaver lived till after the damsons were taken there—he has a greengrocer's shop at No. 14—I searched that house about an hour after.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
WEAVER — GUILTY . Aged 53.— Confined Six Months.
GREEN — GUILTY . Aged 23.—( See the next Case.)
[trialAccount t18431023-3032]
3032. The said JOHN GREEN and JOHN SLAYMAKER were indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of Oct., 1 basket, value 1 s.; and 1 bushel of damsons, 5 s.; the goods of Gabriel Hards, the master of Green.
REBECCA WILLIAMS HARDS . I live with my father, Gabriel Hards, a fruit-salesman in Covent-garden market, and live in Basing-place, Lambeth. About four o'clock in the afternoon of the 2nd of Oct. I was attending to my father's stall—Slaymaker was employed in the next stall—there was a sieve of damsons partly on my father's stall, and partly on the next—Slaymaker came and took up the sieve—Green was at our stall at the time, and I was at the stand outside the shop—Slaymaker put the sieve on his head, went towards Bow-street, and told Green if any one wanted him he should he back in ten minutes—I told Green I did not believe that sieve of damsons belonged to Bedell, which is the name Slaymaker goes by in the market—Green said, "O yes, it does, we have no damsons on that side"—I know we had damsons on that side—my father had permission to place his on that side—I have seen Green emptying them from there, by my father's desire—in a quarter of an hour Slaymaker returned—I asked him if he had not made a mistake in taking that sieve of damsons, as I had noticed that German sieve standing there for some time, and thought it was my father's—he said, "No, they were not, and as for German sieves, I can show you half a score down in the cellar"—the damsons are worth 5 s. a bushel—Weaver's stall was shut up at the time, and no business going on—the shutters were closed.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Slaymaker was in Weaver's service? A. Yes—there were no goods outside Weaver's stalll—this sieve was just behind some tiers of baskets, on the boundary between our stall and
Weaver's—I know there were none of Weaver's on our side, and there were not any open for sale.
MARIA BOSHER . I am the wife of Thomas Bosher, and keep a greengrocer's shop in Drury-lane. Between four and five o'clock, on the 2nd of Oct., I was in the parlour—I saw a man bringing a basket of damsons into the shop—I do not know who it was—I did not expect any damsons—I got out of my parlour to go and see, but the man was gone, leaving the damsons—I do not know Slaymaker—I could not say whether he is the man.
JOSEPH BAXTER . I am a porter in the market. On the 2nd of Oct. I saw Slaymaker take this basket of damsons on his head—he went up Bow-street, and through Long-acre, to Bosher's shop in Drury-lane, and left them there—I have since seen the basket—it is the same.
FREDERICK JOHN JOHNSON . I am a beadle of the market. Between four and five o'clock, on the 2nd of Oct., I saw Slaymaker go across the market, up Bow-street—I went to Bosher's shop, and found a basket of damsons there—I showed them to the prosecutor, and they were given up to him—this is the basket—I asked Slaymaker where he took the sieve of damsons to which I saw him carry up Russell-street—he said he had taken them to Bosher's, in Drury-lane, under the immediate authority of Green—I asked what he was going to receive—he said, "All I shall have will be a drop of gin or a drop of beer for my trouble."
MR. HORRY. Q. Did he use the words "immediate authority?" A. Words to that effect.
JOHN WEAVER . I keep a stall next to the prosecutor. About four o'clock in the afternoon of the 2nd of Oct. my stall was closed for business, and had been for nearly two hours—Slaymaker had no authority to take any damsons from my stall—I had no goods at all between my stall and Mr. Hards'.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you not German sieves in your cellar? A. Not one of my own in my cellar or on my stand—when my stall is closed, other parties use it—Slaymaker occasionally works for other parties.
GABRIEL HARD . I have seen the basket and some damsons—I have no doubt they are mine.
(Slaymaker received a good character; and John Fothergill, a slop-seller, gave Green a good character.)
GREEN — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
SLAYMAKER — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3033]
3033. CHARLES ANDERSON and CHARLES ROCKLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of Oct., 1 bag, value 1 s.; 2 thimbles, 2 s.; 1 pencil-case, 3 s.; 1 tablet, 4 s.; 2 needle-cases, 1 s.; 1 pair of scissors, 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief, 6 d.; the goods of Mary Ann Pearce, from her person; and that Anderson had been before convicted of felony.
MARY ANN PEARCE . I live on Dane-hill, Margate. At half-past eight o'clock at night, on the 5th of Oct., I was walking with Mrs. Taylor, in the New-road, with a bag on my arm, containing two thimbles and the other things stated—I saw Anderson come by me, and another man run across the road—I felt the strings of my bag cut, and my bag was gone—this is it.
ANN TAYLOR . I am the wife of Richard Taylor. At half-past eight o'clock that evening, I was walking with the prosecutrix—I saw the bag on her arm—Anderson passed close by me—the prosecutrix held up her arm, and said, "Oh, that fellow!"—I saw that the strings of her bag were cut—I went immediately across the road after Anderson—he had been near enough to cut the strings—I caught hold of Anderson's arm, and Rockley stopped me—he held
me, and prevented my running after Anderson—he asked me what was the matter—Anderson got away—I have no doubt he is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. This was opposite Judd-street? A. Yes—Anderson had not got above three steps before I went after him—he passed in front of me, and was in front the whole way—he kept in the middle of the road—I saw no omnibus coming, and none interrupted me—I saw no one till Rockley stopped me—I had just got hold of Anderson when I was stopped by Rockley, and Anderson went up Judd-street—I was not detained above a quarter of a minute.
Rockley. Q. When I was at the station, did not you say you could not swear to me? A. No, I swear you are the man who caught my arm, and stopped me from running after Anderson—I have no doubt of you.
WILLIAM PATTERSON ( police-constable E 25.) I heard a cry of "Stop thief," and saw Anderson running—he threw this bag from his hand into an area—it was brought up in my presence—I saw several persons running.
JOHN VESPER ( police-constable E 158.) I saw Anderson running, and a cry of "Stop thief" after him—I took him—the prosecutrix, Mrs. Taylor, and several other persons, came up—I took Anderson to the station—after I had him, Rockley came up, and said Anderson was not the man who committed the offence, that I had got the wrong person in custody—he was secured.
Rockley. At the time of the robbery I was at home in Stanmore-street—I was fetched by Anderson's sister—I went, and was taken.
JOHN ROWE ( police-constable E 91.) I produce a certificate of Anderson's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person.
ANDERSON — GUILTY . Aged 18.
ROCKLEY* — GUILTY . Aged 19.
Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3034]
3034. FRANCIS JACK was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Oct., 1 dead duck, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of Thomas Goddard.
JOHN ROBERT DARK . I am in the service of Thomas Goddard, poulterer, Maida-hill. On the 14th of Oct, between eleven and twelve o'clock at night, the prisoner and another man came to the shop—the other man bought some bacon, and the prisoner took a dead dock—I heard the paper it was on rattle—I looked, and it was gone—I ran after him down North-street, and saw the duck in his hand—I pursued him to Carlile-street, and before I lost sight of him I told him I should see him at Pope's on Monday, and would give him in charge—he dropped the duck—I am sure of that.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not the other man speak some words to him? A. Yes—I saw the prisoner with a duck, talking to a greengrocer—I heard the prisoner ask the green-grocer if he would bring it back, and he said he would not—he offered the duck to me to take back, and I said I would not—he put it under his coat, and then dropped it—he appeared perfectly sober—I had a scuffle with him—he got away—I hallooed after him, and he came back to strike the green-grocer—my master came and held him, and I fetched the policeman—I knew the prisoner when he was at Mr. Charles Brooks's.
EDWARD FOSKETT . I saw the prisoner take the duck, and run away with it.
EDWARD BRIGDEN ( police-constable S 125.) I took the prisoner—he was not drunk.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know that? A. By his appearance.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3035]
3035. ROBERT ROBINSON ALEXANDER was indicted for embeszlement.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES SCOTT . I keep the Uxbridge Arms, at Notting-hill. I deal with Messrs. Strong and Larchin for beer—on the 20th of Sept., I paid the prisoner 19 l. 19 s. 11 d. on account of the firm—he gave me this receipt.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long have you dealt with the firm? A. From the 8th of last March—I knew Mr. Larchin for some years—the prisoner was a collecting clerk—I made his acquaintance through Larebin and Co.
WILLIAM GLASS . I keep the King's Arms beer-shop at Pentonville. I deal with Strong and Larchin—on the 21st of Sept. I paid the prisoner 2 l., and on the 23rd of Sept. 3 l., on account of the firm—this is the prisoner's signature in my book.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you been a customer of the house, introduced by the prisoner? A. Yes—I only knew I was dealing with that house through him—I believe the prisoner kept a beer-shop at Wai worth—the monies I paid him were on account of the goods I had had.
SAMUEL STRONG . I am a brewer, and am in partnership with Henry Larchin and Henry Smith, at Ratcliff-cross—the prisoner was in our service—it was his duty to receive money, collect orders, and to account to me for the money he received—he had an allowance of ten per cent, and twopence in the pound for expenses—there is no commission remaining due to him on any monies that he has paid—he has never accounted to me for 19 l. 19 s. 11 d. from Mr. Scott, on the 20th of Sept.—on the following day I learned he had received 20 l., and I asked him the reasons he had not paid it, with other money he had paid on the Wednesday—he said he had not enough by 1 l., and he would bring it on Saturday—he promised to call with it—I did not see him afterwards—he has never accounted to me for 2 l. and 3 l., received from Mr. Glass on the 21st and 23rd of Sept.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you make any inquiry whether he had received these two sums? A. I had not an opportunity of doing so—I did not see him afterwards—he solicited us to employ him—we did not go to him—there was no writing executed between us—I told him he ought to keep a book to enter the money, and, in fact, he had a book when he brought the first money—he was to pay me once or twice a week—not less than once a week certainly—there was an understanding about that.
Q. Have you not been in correspondence with his attorney, and found, on application, that 60 l. was claimed for balance due from you to him? A. No; no claim of 60 l. has been made on us by Salmon and Long, on his account—we received a letter on the 8th or 9th of Oct. from them, in which they say that there was a large sum due, but they do not state the amount—that was before I gave the prisoner into custody—his attorney made a demand on us for money, in answer to a letter we wrote to the prisoner, addressed to his own house—I rather think we gave him into custody the day after we received the letter—I will not swear when we received the letter—I believe it was not nearly a week after we received it that we gave him in charge, but I cannot tell—he was not to receive a commission upon all the custom he got, but upon all the monies he paid in, because there were a great many bad debts—I believe he went about to the publicans, and drank with them to get custom—I settled with him last on the 20th of Sept.—here is the book—I never had a general account—I always paid him as he went on—I received 5 l. 19 s. 10 d. on the 20th of Sept, as a balance of an account of Scott and Glass—I do not know when I had received any money before that had any reference to Scott—I have received from the prisoner, in
the course of twelve months, 600 l. or 700 l.—it was not 1000 l.—the first time he paid me money, I told him he ought to keep a book, and, in fact he did then—I certainly saw the book two or three times—it is four or five months since I saw it—I cannot recollect when it was—I was in the habit of entering in my book all the money I received from him—I will swear I always entered it in the course of the day—I may have taken the money, and put it in my iron chest, and entered it the same day, perhaps half a dozen times, but have not entered it at the moment—I have not done so twenty times—I have received lumping sums from him on account—in Sept. I received 25 l. of him through Cook, and that he balanced with me on the 20th of Sept.—here it is in the book—I had received the 25 l. of him before that, and we balanced it on that day—I do not know what period of the supply of goods this 25 l. ran over—I asked him about this 19 l. 19 s. 11 d. on Thursday the 21st—I think I have not stated that it was several days after—I believe I said it was the next day—the 20 l. of Mr. Scott's ran over a period of a fortnight—we never settled with Mr. Scott at all—it was a running account—it was on the 21st that I received an account that he had received money—I do not know whether he remained at home from then till the 10th of Oct.—we sent for him two or three times—we sent Mr. Cook, and I believe he saw him—I never was at his house but once, that might be on the 26th—I believe in answer to that call we had a letter—we let the prisoner be at large two or three days after that—we consulted our attorney—here is a small paper on which I settled an account—here is 36 l. 16 l., 1 l. 16 s. poundage to the customer, and 4 l. 0 s. 2 d. for his expenses—it is not marked expenses—it is my writing—the last I received was this 25 l., about the 18th of Sept.—I did not swear it was the 20th—the balance was settled on the 20th—it was on the 18th be paid that 25 l.—Mr. Cook has the memorandum that he received 25 l.—Mr. Collins, Mr. Cook, and Mr. Larchin, used to receive money on account, as well as I did—I was not present when the prisoner was taken—he did not claim a commission on such beer as we sold to customers whom he had introduced—he did not say, that after he got an order or two, instead of sending him I went myself—we have furnished beer to Mr. Lane, since he introduced him to us, to the amount of 30 l., and have received 34 l. from Mr. Curtis—I do not believe the prisoner had anything to do with Mr. Beal—I think we have received 40 l. from Mr. Scott—we have not received 43 l. from Mr. Jones—there is an account—I am sorry to say that we have not received 30 l. from Mr. Miller, there is not 30 l. due—he received 10 l.—I believe there in 30 l. due from Mr. Glass.
COURT. Q. Did the prisoner make a claim against you before you took him into custody? A. Yes, but he never made any claim himself—we first made our claim on him on the 3rd of Oct.—we sent to him repeatedly.
MR. HORRY. Q. You sent to him? A. Yes—he sent a person to say he would come, but he did not—this memorandum was made before him—the sums which have been mentioned just now have been received since he was in custody—excepting these sums, there is no other claim for commission against us—he was not entitled to receive any commission till the money was paid—we always allowed it to him at the time he paid the money—if he had handed money to any one of the clerks, or to Mr. Larchin, it was his duty to account to me.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 41.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18431023-3036]
3036. WILLIAM NUTTALL was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of Oct., 56lbs. weight of lead, value 7 s., the goods of John Till; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
EDWARD CHAPMAN . I am a coach-maker, and live in Harrow-street. I was employed at some premises in Park-street, Camden-town—the prisoner was on the premises when I went there—I took off three pieces of lead—one piece I threw down into the yard, the other two laid on the party wall—that was about eleven or twelve o'clock, on the 3rd of Oct.—about five I received a communication from the policeman—I then missed the lead.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Which did you miss? A. The piece that was thrown into the yard—this is it—I know it by the way it was rolled up.
JAMES HARROD ( police-constable S 179.) On the 3rd of Oct. I was on duty in Park-street, opposite the gateway of Mr. Till—I saw the prisoner come from the premises with this lead on his shoulder—I communicated with Harding.
JOHN HARDING ( police-constable S 166.) I had information from Harrod—I went to a marine-store dealers, and saw the prisoner in the shop—I kept where they could not see me—I saw the marine-store dealer put some money on the counter, and this lead was in the scale—after the prisoner left I asked where he got the lead—he said where he was putting down other—I asked who authorised him—he said the foreman—he went back to the premises, and said the foreman was not there, but there was a man on the roof would make it all right—he asked if I was on duty, and said, "You need not see it—it is all right;"—in going to the station, he said he had made a fool of himself a good many times, but he thought he had done so once too often now.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was it he went to? A. To Mr. Tili's yard in Park-street, Camden-town—the man came off the roof—I asked if he had authorised this man to sell any lead—he said no, he had desired him in the morning to take nothing from the premises.
WILLIAM HOGBEN . I am foreman to Mr. John Till—these premises in Park-street are his—I did not authorise the prisoner to take the lead.
JOSEPH WALKER ( police-sergeant D 5.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the man.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined One Year.

3038. JOHN JOHNSON and JAMES HALEY were indicted for stealing, on the 13th of Oct., 49lbs. weight of nails, value 2 l.; the goods of William Reid and others: and that Haley had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN FIELD . I am a veterinary surgeon, employed on the premises of William Reid and others, in Liquorpond-street—they had some nails of a particular sort—I lost some of them, to the value of about 40 s.—I beleive these produced are them—they correspond in every respect.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What nails are they? A. For horses shoes—I gave the description for the pattern of them—I have never seen any like them—I cannot say when I had last seen any of these—we have missed nails for the last two years; but I cannot say that all these left at one time.
LEWIS DURANT . I am foreman to Mr. Branscomb, an ironmonger—we supply the prosecutors with these nails—they are made for no one but them.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you not reason to believe that there are other nails of this sort made? A. I do not think there are—they are drawn from under the head very differently to what the generality of nails are.
HENRY REDMAN ( police-constable G 140.) I stopped the prisoners with these nails in Goswell-street, on the evening of the 13th of October—Haley had them on his head—I asked Johnson if they belonged to him—he said they did, and that he bdught horse-parings—Haley said he worked for Johnson.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say he bought horse-parings all over London? A. Yes, and that he picked the nails out of the horse-parings—I believe these nails have not been on a horse's foot.
JOHN FIELD re-examined. I believe people in shoeing horses, drop some nails on the ground—a poor man going about buying the horse-parings might get a few nails, but very few, and they would not be in this condition—a few of these are rusty, but not many.
Haley's Defence. I am in the employ of Johnson; we travel all over London; I have the sifting of the parings, and I put the new nails on one side, the old ones on the other, and take them to a farrier's shop and sell them.
JOSEPH GINGER ( police-constable G 137.) I produce a certificate of Haley's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
JOHNSON — GUILTY . Aged 36.— Confined Six Months.
HALEY — GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined One Year.

JOSEPH DONALDSON . I am in the employ of Mr. Haywood, a pawnbroker. I took in these articles of Smith, on the 16th.

3040. JANE MALLARD was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 23rd of Oct., 3dwts. of gold, value 10 s. 6 d., the goods of Henry Williamson, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
HENRY WILLIAMSON . I live in Perceval-street, Clerkenwell. I had a boy in my employ—I charged him with stealing some gold from me—in consequence of what he said, I went to the prisoner's house, in Fox-place, Clerkenwell, where the boy lived—I showed the prisoner the gold I lost, and the said she had picked up a piece like that in Clerkenwell-close, and sold it to Mr. Hearn for 10 s.
RICHARD LIDGETT . I lived with Mr. Williamson. He charged me with stealing gold—I took three bits, at three different times, home to the prisoner, and she told me she sold them—the first morning I took it she gave me 6 d.—I took a bit on the Saturday morning, and she gave me 1 s.—I told her I had got it away from my place.
CHARLES SAUNDERS . I am shopman to Mr. Hearn. I bought a piece of gold, but I cannot say of whom—I buy pieces of gold of poor women like the prisoner without knowing who they are.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going up Clerkenwell-close, and picked up a piece of blue paper, and in it was a thin piece of gold; I did not know what it was; I took it to this young man, and asked him; he said it was gold; when Lidgett came home I told him, and he said, "You ought to give me part of it."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 53.— Confined Six Months.

3043. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of Oct., 1 truck, value 8 l.; 7 1/2 lbs. weight of tea, 1 l. 17 s.; 71bs. weight of coffee, 12 s. 10 d.; 69lbs. weight of tea, 2 l.; 10lbs. weight of candles, 4 s. 6 d.; 1lb. weight of rice, 5 d.; and 3/4 lb. weight of cocoa, 7 d.; the goods of Richard Sexton and another; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
RICHARD SEXTON . I am a grocer, and live in High-street, Newington. I had a truck on the 12th of Oct. at my house—it contained 7 1/2 lbs. of tea, and the other things stated—I sent the whole of them in the truck, by Edward Copas, to take to my different customers—I saw the truck the next day at the station, with the different articles.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you put the articles up? A. I put up some, and my man put up the others—I superintended it—I have one partner.
EDWARD COPAS . I went to Mr. Huntley's, in Pump-row, Old-street, and began to deliver some of these things, and, while I was inside, a man took the truck, and the property—this is it.
Cross-examined. Q. Had you delivered any of the order? A. Yes, three parcels—I left the truck for about ten minutes, and nobody with it—it was about five o'clock.
JAMES WHITCOMBE . I live with my father, in Norris-street, Hoxton, I saw Copas go into the house—I then saw the prisoner take the truck away, with these things in it—there was a bag in it—he took it out, carried it through the street, rested at a public-house, and then took it away with him.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I work for a cabinet carver—I was sitting on some logs in Pump-row—was out for my tea half-hour—I had never seen the prisoner before—I am quite sure he is the person—I saw him for about half an hour—after I had told Copas, I looked for a policeman—I found two, and told them—they went after the prisoner, and caught him with the bag—I had not lost sight of him—he was on the other side of the way.
JOHN JONES ( policeman.) Whitcombe told me that a man had stolen the bag off the truck, and said, "Yonder he goes"—he pointed the prisoner out to me and the other policeman—we took him with it—the prisoner appeared quite sober.
DANIEL BLAY ( City police-constable No. 159.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3044]
3044. CLARA LEWIS and JAMES GLENDON were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of Oct., 1 watch, value 6 l.; 1 watch chain, 15 s.; and 1 watchkey, 5 s.; the goods of Samuel Irvine.
SAMUEL IRVINE . I am butler to Samuel Duckworth, Esq. A few minutes before one o'clock, on the 18th of October, my watch, with a gold chain and seal, was on a nail over the fire-place in the pantry, which is about fire yards from the area door—I returned in half an hour, and they were gone—this is the watch-chain and seal.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What time had you seen it safe? A. A few minutes before one—the bell rang, and the kitchen-maid went up to answer it—the watch must have been taken about that period—I do not believe that while the kitchen-maid went up, the party came and committed the robbery—there was another person taken up, but not in consequence of what the kitchen-maid said—she could not identify that person.
WILLIAM EDWARD VINRON . I am shopman to a pawnbroker. This watch was pawned on the 18th of Oct., before four o'clock, by the prisoner Lewis.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know her? A. Yes; she frequently pledges—I do not know that she keeps a bad house—I offered her 2 l. for the watch—she then said I knew whose it was, but did not mention the name of any person—I do not recollect her saying that a gentleman in her house asked her to pawn it.
JOSEPH KENT ( policeman.) I took Lewis—I told her it was for pledging this watch—she said, when at the station-house, that she had pledged it.
HENRY WYATT . I am shopman to a pawnbroker. This seal, chain, and key, were pawned by Glendon on the 18th of Oct., about three o'clock.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know there was another person with him? A. Yes; because when I asked him for a halfpenny for the ticket, he said he would go and get it—he stepped out and got a pennypiece, some person must have given it him.
MR. BALLANTINE called
JULIA PHILLIPS . I am the wife of Mr. Charles Phillips, a bootmaker, in Fleet-street, Blackfriars—I know the prisoner Lewis—I do not know what sort of a house she keeps—I live a few doors off her—she was taken up on
a Thursday—I went to her house at eleven o'clock on the day before—I was mending a counterpane, and I took it there to sit with Lewis—I am positive I got there as near eleven o'clock as could be—Glendon was there when I went—he went out, and returned about half-past twelve—from that time I did not lose sight of them at all, till I left at two o'clock.
COURT. Q. How came you to remember the day? A. It was the day before they were taken.
LOUISA JOHNSON . I lodge at Lewis's—Glendon lives there—I remember seeing Lewis the day before she was taken up—I got up at eight o'clock in the morning, and was in Lewis's company all day long—the was not out from twelve o'clock till two—I saw Glendon there—he went out at a quarter to twelve, and returned at half-past—I saw him there during the remainder of the day.
COURT. Q. You know them both very well, do you? A. Yes; I remember the day, because it was the day before they were taken.
CHARLES SMITH . I know Lewis's house—I remember Wednesday the 18th of Oct.—the prisoners were taken on the Thursday—on that Wednesday I was at their house from about ten minutes to one till half-past, or from twenty minutes to half-past—I saw them both there.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18431023-3045]
3045. JOHN HARDEN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Oct., 1 sextant, value 5 l.; the goods of Samuel Snell, in a vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
BARTHOLOMEW WILLIAM RIDDELL . I am steward of the barque the Dale Park, which was in the West India Dock—I saw-the sky-light safe about twenty minutes past five, on the 20th of Oct.—the watchman came to me about ten minutes to six—the sky-light was then off, and the sextant was gone—it belonged to the captain, Samuel Snell—it has not been found since—the sideboard drawer was open, and other things were gone.
THOMAS GLADWELL . I am an officer. At a quarter-past six that day the prisoner came to the dock-gate, and presented me with a pass for a sextant—I heard something rattle in it, and I said, "What is this?"—he said, "Some of the glasses are broken, I am going to get it repaired; I am learning navigation"—I believed, from the pass, that be belonged to a vessel—he was coming out of Limehouse basin entrance—there were a great many people came out, but none with a sextant.
Prisoner's Defence. The person who opens the door at the Haymarket, can prove I was at the theatre door that evening before the gates were open; I went in, and after the first act I came out, and went to a public-house; the man said, "Can I have half a pint of beer with you;" I said, "Yes."
GUILTY .

THOMAS DOWNER . I am a constable of the docks. At half-past six o'clock, on the 23rd of Oct., the prisoner was coming out with this cruetstand, and this paper purporting to be a pass—I took him.
HENRY PAINTER . I am steward of the Hopewell. This cruet-stand belongs to Captain James Gilbert O'Leason—it was safe after five o'clock, on the 23rd of Oct.—the prisoner had no business with it.

3049. HENRY CURRALL and GEORGE PRICE were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Oct., 1 bottle, value 2 d.; and 1 pint of pickled onions, 1 s.; the goods of Hannah Swan.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) I was on duty on the 14th of Oct.—I saw the prisoners with four other persons walking three by three—I knew them, and watched them—I saw Price go into the prosecutor's shop—Currall put his arm into the shop, and he came away with this bottle in his arms—Price said to one of the others, "I have got it"—I secured Currall in the act of giving the bottle to Price.
JOHN RICHARD RICHARDS . This bottle of pickles is the property of my sister Hannah Swan—I live with her.

3051. JAMES FLETCHER, GEORGE MEADOWS , and RICHARD MEADOWS were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of Oct., 31lbs. weight of hair, value 1 l.; the goods of Benjamin Carr, the master of Fletcher, and George Meadows.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
BENJAMIN CARR . I am a horse-hair manufacturer, and live in Banner-street, St. Luke's. Fletcher and George Meadows worked in my factory—I rent a place in Golden-lane, where the hair was taken from—on the 16th of Oct. I received a letter—in consequence of that I conferred with the police—the next day I saw a bag containing some hair—it had been taken from Golden-lane—Richard Meadows had a right on the premises at Golden-lane—Fletcher had no business there whatever—I had ordered my foreman on Tuesday morning to let George take this hair to Golden-lane, to have it carded by a steam-press I have there, and to bring it back—he had no right to dispose of it.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long has Fletcher been with you? A. About eleven years—he bore an excellent character—four or fire months back he asked me to let him have a bale of hair for his son to work at home—I let him have it—he has bought hair of me five or six times—I have two places of business—I rent a steam-press in Golden-lane—the foreman was stationed in Banner-street.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Richard was in your employ? A. He had been for three or four years, but was not at this time—I should have had no objection to his calling on his brother there.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police sergeant G 20.) In consequence of directions from the prosecutor, I went on Tuesday, the 14th of Oct., to a court leading into Golden-lane, where I could watch the entrance leading into the factory—I saw Fletcher standing at the corner of Arthur-street-leading to the prosecutor's factory—I pointed him out to Cole—he had no appearance of bulk about him—he went towards the factory—in three-quarters of an hour he came from the entrance of the court—there was no thoroughfare, so that he must have come from the factory—he had a bag nearly full—he passed me with it in Play-house-yard—I watched outside a public-house—he went in, and Redman followed George and Fletcher into the house—I had seen George three or four times before Fletcher went into the house—(I had first seen George coming from the prosecutor's residence in Banner-street, about 300 yards off—he went to the factory)—they staid in the public-house three or four minutes—Redman came out before them—George came towards me, and went towards the City, and I followed Fletcher with Redman—he turned round in Chis well-street, saw me, and went into a public-house—I followed, told him I belonged to the police, and wanted to know where he got this hair—he said, "I bought it in the Curtain-road"—I said, "How came you this way from the Curtain-road?"—he said, "Oh, this I bought in Smithfield"—I said, "Which way did you come?"—he said, "Through Long-lane and Barbican"—I asked if he had called anywhere—he at first said no—I asked if he did not call on some one in Golden-lane—he said, "Yes, I did"—I found this hair in the bag.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was Richard admitted to bail on the first occasion? A. Yes.
HENRY REDMAN ( police-constable C 24.) I was with Brannan and Cole—I saw George Meadows come from the entrance of the factory into Golden-lane—he looked right and left, as if looking who were about—Fletcher then came from the entrance of the factory, and met George—they went into a public-house at the left hand side of Golden-lane—Fletcher had a bag—it was such a sized bag as George must have seen it—George did not have the bag—I followed them into the public-house—Fletcher put the bag down—they
had a pint of porter and half-a-quartern of gin—Fletcher then took the bag across his shoulder—George wished him good day, and said, "I shall see you again to-morrow"—I took George—he said he knew nothing about the bag or the stealing of it.
ROBERT COLE ( policeman.) I was with Brannan and Redman—I saw Fletcher going to the factory without anything—after that I saw George come from the factory, followed by Fletcher, who was carrying a bag—I went back to the factory, and found Richard there, and a key on him—I fitted that key to the padlock of the warehouse the hair was in, and it opened it with ease.
BENJAMIN CARR re-examined. I believe this hair to be mine—it is impossible to swear to it—it is just the kind that was ordered to be given out that day—George ought to have brought back some hair to my house in Banner-street—he would not be doing right in taking it anywhere else—he had no right to be bringing this hair back—it is just as it was sent there—this is my bag, but on one occasion my foreman lent Fletcher a bag to carry hair in, and'it might be this bag.
FLETCHER — GUILTY . Aged 48.
GEORGE MEADOWS — GUILTY . Aged 27.
Recommended to mercy.
Confined Six Months.
RICHARD MEADOWS — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18431023-3052]
3052. THOMAS ASHMORE was indicted for feloniously receving, on the 10th of Jan., 9 shirts, value 2 l. 19 s. 6 d.; and 1 scarf, 5 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Mills; well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN MILLS . I am a hosier, and live in Oxford-street. About two years ago I bought eleven shirts of one Weston—I saw them in my possession last about twelve months ago—I cannot say when I had the scarfs safe, because I have so many of the same description—I never sold the shirts.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. Had you a man in your employ of the name of Gravel? A. Yes, till last April—he robbed me to a very great extent—myself, a boy, and a young man serve in my shop.
WILLIAM WESTON . I am a manufacturer of shirts. About two years and a half ago I had shirts in my possession—I know this one—I sold it to be prosecutor—there were eleven of this kind—this one has my own mark on it.
Cross-examined. Q. What is there particular about it? A. It is marked outside—it has a calico body, with linen collar, front, and wrists—I may sell 200 dozen of these shirts in the course of a year—I know I sold this to Mr. Mills, because I have referred to my book—my brother, who is dead, made the entry—I took them in from Boswell, who was then the work-woman—I know that, because I put the mark on it, and wrote "Boswell" on it—she has not worked for roe since, because she spoiled these goods—she had made hundreds before—all the shirts that come from her do not bear her mark, unless they are badly made—this is the only shirt I speak to.
JOHN ANDERTON . I am out of business. About the 10th of Jan. the prisoner brought me nine shirts, and asked if I would purchase them—I asked the price—he said, 3 s. 6 d. a-piece—I gave it him—this is the bill and receipt he gave me in his own handwriting, it is 1 l. 11 s. 6 d.—about two months after I saw him again, in the King of Prussia public-house, in Somerset-place, Hoxton-town—he asked what I meant to do—I said, "I don't know, you know what you have done, you must do the best you can"—he said he was going to surrender, and asked if I was going to say what I gave for them—I
said yes, I was—these shirts have been so long out of ray possession, I cannot say whether they are what I bought of him—to the best of my belief they are—I kept them by me for some time, and gave three of them to Gladwell to pledge.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you by trade? A. A pawnbroker, but I have been out of that business some years—I was in the greengrocery trade—I am now living on my property—I did not fail as a pawnbroker—I did not like the business, and when I was out of my time I did not do any more—I pawned these shirts for 6 s.—this bill is in the same state as when I got it from the prisoner—it has never been out of my possession, nor been touched by me or any one else—this writing, and one figure above another, was on it when I received it—the prisoner first put down 2 s. 6 d.—I said, "You have made a mistake," and he altered it to 3 s. 6 d.—as far as I can recollect, it was on the 9th of Jan. I bought them.
SARAH JANE GLADWELL . I live in Canal-road, Hoxton. Anderton gave me three shirts, and I pawned them at Mr. Leech's, Hoxton, for 6 s.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you a widow? A. Yes—I took the things to pay myself, because Anderton owed me money for washing—I gave the ticket to Anderton.
HENRY LIEBRECHT . I live in Bethnal-green. I was in the service of Mr. Sowerby, late Leech, of Hoxton—I took in these three shirts of Gladwell.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you particularly notice them? A. No more than to see that they were of the value asked.
GEORGE KEMP ( policeman.) On the 27th of April I went to Someset-place, Hoxton, and watched a bouse to see a party who has since been convicted—I saw that party and the prisoner come out of the house together—they turned round and saw me—I saw tht prisoner, and knew him—I went and changed my dress—I was in private clothes—I then followed them to Clerkenwell—I had at that time these five shirts in my possession—I showed them to Mr. Weston and Mr. Mills.
WILLIAM EDWARD BALL ( policeman.) On the 27th of April I saw the prisoner—I saw him again on the 13th of Oct., in Shoreditch, and apprehended him.
JOHN MILLS re-examined. To the best of my belief, these are the shirts I bought of Weston.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18431023-3053]

3054. TIMOTHY COLE was again indicted for stealing, on the 9th of Oct., 3 spoons, value 8 s.; and 1 pair of sugar-tongs, 4 s.; the goods of William Gear.
WILLIAM GEAR . I keep a coffee-shop, in Bury-street, Bloomsbury. The prisoner was in the habit of coming to my shop—these are my spoons and tongs—I saw the prisoner at my shop about the 9th or 10th of Oct.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What do you know them by? A. By the marks "E. B. 1780," on two of them—the other is not sufficiently engraved-the tongs have "1793" on them.
HENRY LANGHAM . I am in the employ of David Jones, a pawnbroker, in High Holborn. On the 10th of Oct. the prisoner offered these sugartongs to me—I asked who they belonged to—he said to hit father, who lived
Museum-street—I went and found no one lived there of that name—I afterwards saw him near my shop, and gave him into custody.
Cross-examine. Q. Had you ever seen him before? A. No—he was in our shop five minutes—I went out, and he went out too, and when I came back he was standing a few houses off.
WILLIAM SINNOCK ( policeman.) I went to Mr. Jones's shop at three o'clock in the afternoon, and found the prisoner—he was given into custody—I found the spoons on him—he said they were his father's, and he did not wish him to know it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Four Months.

3056. WILLIAM ROWLAND was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Oct., 33lbs. weight of paper, value 4 s., the goods of John Conrad Kohler.
JOHN CONRAD KOHLER . I am an oilman, and live in Marylebone-street. The prisoner was employed in painting my house—I had some waste paper in a loft at the top of the house, in packages similar to these—the prisoner had access to it—He was there two or three days—Here is 13lbs. of paper.
Cross-examined by MR. CROUCH. Q. Have you got much paper in this loft? A. Yes, four or five hundred weight—I cannot say positively whether I have missed any, nor that this is the paper—my family were in the house—I believe there was another man in the house besides the prisoner—I have known him some time—his family are respectable.
COURT. Q. What time used he to come to work? A. About a quarter past six, and went away about six in the evening.
JOHN GRAY ( police-constable C 14.) On the 7th of Oct. I was in Rupert-street, Haymarket, and saw the prisoner with two baskets containing this paper—he went into a marine-store shop in Rupert-street, remained there about two minutes, and came out—I followed him to Wardour-street, and asked what he had got in his basket—he said, "Books, which I got from a young man at Hampstead"—I took him, and he said, "You knew me, and suspected me; I got the paper from the top of Mr. Kohler's house in Marylebone, where I was at work."
Cross-examined. Q. Did you tell him to confess? A. No—he did not offer to run away—he said he would go with me.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3057]
3057. JOHN MARTIN was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of October, 9 yards of printed cotton, value 5 s., the goods of Thomas Morgan.
WILLIAM HAYWARD . I am a porter, and live in Lyall-row, Red Lion Market. About half-past nine o'clock at night, on the 14th of Oct., I saw the prisoner and a girl about seventeen or eighteen years old, standing outside Mr. Morgan's window—the girl spoke to the prisoner, and he went into the shop—she remained outside—she tapped at the window, and gave some sign to the prisoner, and then she went in—I went to the window, and saw Mr. Morgan showing the girl a blue shawl—I saw the prisoner come out—he had got hold of the side of his coat—he laughed coming out, ran into the road, and ran as fast as he could—I ran after him—when I got near him he turned the first turning from Old-street, and saw there was some one pursuing—he threw this cotton between my legs—I took it up, and called, "Stop thief"—I took him and brought him back to the shop—I gave the piece of print to Mrs. Morgan.
MARY MORGAN . I am the wife of Thomas Morgan, a linen-draper, in Old-street. This is my husband's print—the prisoner came in and asked the price
of a shawl—he called a girl in afterwards—I showed her the shawl, and told her the price—I went to get some other shawls down—the prisoner went out, and the girl followed him shortly after—I did not see the prisoner again till he was brought back by Hay ward about five minutes after—this print was in the shop that day—it is my husband's.
JOSEPH BRYANT ( policeman.) The prisoner was given into my custody—at the station he said some girl wished him to take it.
GUILTY. Aged 12.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.

3058. WILLIAM BURKE was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Oct., 24 cigars, value 6 s., the goods of Salmon Salmon.
RICHARD WITHERS . At a quarter to twelve o'clock on the morning of the 23rd of Oct., I was in Great Turnstile—I saw the prisoner and two others standing at Mr. Salmon's shop—one of them said, "Go in, it is all right"—the young man was cleaning the window—the prisoner went in and helped himself to some cigars on the counter—he put them into his breeches' pocket and came out—the others followed him—they met together in Holborn, and divided the cigars between them—I saw a policeman, and told him—he took the prisoner—the others escaped.
EVE SALMON . I am the wife of Salmon Salmon, a tobacconist, in Great Turnstile. About eleven o'clock, in the morning of the 23rd, I went into the back parlour while the men were cleaning the windows—no one was in the shop—soon after the prisoner was brought back—I looked into a box on the counter, which had had two dozen cigars in it, and found only three left—these are the same sort of cigars.
JAMES WRIGHT ( police-constable F 54) I received information, and took the prisoner—I found these two cigars on him.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Two Months.

3059. GEORGE CHAPPELL was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of Oct., 30 yards of cloth, called Alpaca, value 3 l., the goods of Christopher Leaf.
THOMAS HILL . I am porter to Christopher Leaf, of Wood-street. Between two and three o'clock in the afternoon of the 17th of Oct., the prisoner came, and inquired if our salesman was in the warehouse—I said no, he was at dinner—he said he would call again; and, in going out, I saw him take this piece of goods—I went and overtook him in Mumford-court, about 300 yards from my master's, with this cloth, which is ours.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANS. Q. Do not you know that he had dealings with the salesman? A. Yes.

3060. JOHN KENNEDY and TIMOTHY BRYAN were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of Sept., at St. Martin-in-the-fields, 47 3/4 yards of kerseymere, value 16 s., the goods of John Pannett Bull, in his dwelling-house; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
EDWIN MUNDAY . I am in the service of Mr. John Pannett Bull, woollen warehouseman, in St. Martin's-lane. On Monday, the 4th of Sept., in consequence of information from Collier, I counted the rolls of drab-coloured
cloth, and missed one piece, numbered "8527," from about forty feet from the entrance—it contained 47 3/4 yards, and was worth about 16 l.—it had come from the cloth-workers on the 29th of Aug.—on the 14th of Sept. it was produced by the policeman, and I knew it by the ticket, which was still on it.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You never saw it after the 4th? A. I do not know that I ever saw it at all on my master's premises—I only know it was there from the books—this is the book—it is not in my handwriting,
GEORGE WOOD . I am in the prosecutor's service. He does not live in the house—all the young men do—it is in the parish of St. Martin-in-the-Fields—this ticket is my handwriting—it was on the piece of goods when I wrote it.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not know the goods, do you? A. I do not know the colour, nor to what piece of cloth the ticket was attached—I wrote it about the 30th of Aug.—I marked a great many pieces that day, and this was one of them—there is no date upon it.
SARAH COLLIER . I am the wife of Joseph Collier, a tailor, in Porter-street, Newport-market. Between one and two o'clock, on the 4th of Sept., I was passing along Rose-street, Long-acre, and saw Bryan and another man, (not Kennedy,) in a sailor's dress, rather bow-knee'd—the sailor had a roll of cloth—Bryan was in front of the other—I heard the sailor say to Bryan, D—n his eyes if he would carry it any further, he should be nibbled —Bryan said, "D—n your fool's eyes, come along, it is but a step or two further"—they went through the narrow courts, and Bryan tucked the corner of the cloth in that was hanging down—they rolled the cloth into No. 10, Laaenby-court, and then both ran down Conduit-court.
Cross-examined. Q. Where do you live? A. I did live at No. 2, Lazenby-court, close by—I stood at my door, saw where they put the cloth, and watched them—I told my husband directly, and then I went down to Mr. Bull, to see if he had lost it—Bryan was dressed very differently to what he is now—I have seen him dressed nearly the same as he was that day—he had on a pair of dark trowsers, a waistcoat with red on a black ground, and a blouse—I have seen him in a blouse before—I had never spoken to him before.
JOSEPH COLLIER . I am the husband of Sarah Collier. On the 4th of Sept., in consequence of what she said, I went into Rose-street to look for a policeman—on my return I saw four persons in Lazenby-court—Kennedy was one—they had a bundle in a bag, and went along Hart-street—I followed them to Bull-inn-court, then one of them carried the bundle into a public-house, and the others went towards the Strand—I fetched an officer, went into the public-house, and found the bag behind the door—I took it to the station, and found this roll of cloth in it.
Cross-examined. Q. You will not say that Bryan was one of the four men? A. No, he was not one.
ELIZABETH TAYLOR . I am the wife of James Taylor, and live in Boll Inn-court. On the 4th of Sept., between one and two o'clock, I was at my door—I saw two young men pass down the court—Kennedy was one of them—they came into the narrow part of the court, and ran down into the Strand—the other man was dressed in a frock-coat and a broad hat.
GEORGE WESTON ( police-constable F 6.) I received a roll of cloth from Hunt, and delivered it to Mr. Bull—I cut this piece off that has the ticketon it—I took Kennedy—he denied the charge.
GEORGE HUNT ( police-constable T 26.) I took the cloth from the public-house, and took it to the station.
JOSEPH MOUNT ( police-constoble C 6.) I produce a certificate of Bryan's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
KENNEDY — NOT GUILTY .
BRYAN — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.

3062. REBECCA BROOKS was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of Oct., 10 yards of printed cotton, value 8 s. 6 d.; the goods of Thomas Deverell and another.
SAMUEL HARRIS . I am assistant to Thomas and John Deverell, drapers, in Oxford-street. On the 9th of Oct. the prisoner came into the shop, and while the young man turned to reach down some goods that she asked for, I saw her take this print and put under her shawl—I let her go to the door, and told the young man to fetch her back—he did so, and this dress was taken from under her shawl, in the shop.
