3063. THOMAS WHEELER and TIMOTHY KEITH were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Oct., 1 sovereign; the money of Ann Maria Lyons.
ANN MARIA LYONS . I am a tobacconist. At half-past five o'clock on the 14th of Oct., the prisoners came for a cigar—Keith put down a sovereign, and asked for change—I asked if they had got any change—they both said they had not—I took out my purse, put down a half-sovereign, two half-crowns, four, shillings, two fourpenny-pieces, and took two penny-pieces out of the till—I tried the sovereign; it was a good one, and heavy, a George and dragon one—I got a piece of paper, lit it, and handed it to Wheeler; and while he lighted his cigar, I took up the sovereign, and found it was a counterfeit, and very light—I went round, and accused them of making a mistake—they said, how dare I charge them?—I called my young woman, and she said it was a bad one—I desired her to fetch a policeman—I shot the door—Keith seized my hand and my throat—Wheeler said to him, "Give her the good one, or give me the change"—I heard a something rattling in Keith's mouth—I then found my breath nearly gone, and screamed "Murder"—I threw the counterfeit sovereign from me, and it was picked up, and found where I threw it—this is it—I marked it at the station.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. How long have you kept this
shop? A. More than a twelvemonth—I am a widow—my husband did not keep a shop—I never take a sovereign up till I have given change—I took it up, looked at it, and drew it a little nearer to me—I have a till, but I always put gold into my purse—I took some of the silver out of my purse at that time, because I had not sufficient in the till—I took the first sovereign, and looked at it—I am sure this is not the same—the first was a good one—I rung that—I did not ring the second—no good sovereign was found on the prisoners that I am aware of.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did they appear a little the worse for drink? A. No, not to me—they were in the shop ten minutes or a quarter of an hour.
JOHN DUNN ( police-constable F 46.) I arrived at the shop. The prisoners had hold of Mrs. Lyons—I laid hold of both of them, and told them to pick the sovereign up—they picked it up and gave it to me—I put it into my pocket—I said I must take them to Bow-street—they made a desperate struggle with me in the shop, and the prosecutrix locked the door till we got more assistance—I saw keith swallowing something—there was a lump in his throat.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Is not this shop rather a doubtful place? A. Not that I am aware of—I am the policeman on the beat—I have been there a week or two—I called there this morning for the witness—I do not know what is transacted in the house.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You found on Keith a fourpennypiece? A. Yes, two half-crowns; and four shillings and twopence on Wheeler.
WHEELER* — GUILTY . Aged 23.
KEITH* — GUILTY . Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3064]
3064. WILLIAM HUMPHREYS was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of Oct., 2 umbrellas, value 5 s.; the goods of Thomas Howard.
THOMAS HOWARD . On the 3rd of Oct. I put up my chaise, with two umbrellas in it, at the King's Arms, Fulbam—I missed them when I got home—the prisoner was assisting the ostler at the King's Arms.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Had you seen him before? A. No; he put my horse to about eight in the evening—the umbrellas were taken out of my chaise by the ostler, and I found fault with him for it—I was only going to stop a short time—I cannot tell whether they were put back—these are the umbrellas.
JOHN GODFREY . These umbrellas were pawned by the prisoner at Mr. Watts's—this is the duplicate of them.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you certain of him? A. Yes; he came before serving time in the morning, and I noticed him.
LUKE BUTT ( police-constable B 47.) I took the prisoner—he put his hand into his waistcoat pocket, and I took this duplicate from him.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Three Months.

3065. JOHN ELGER, the younger, was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Sept., 4 carpet bags, value 18 s.; the goods of Edward Smith, his master; and JOHN ELGER , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to be stolen; against the Statute, &c.
EDWARD SMITH . I am a warehouseman in Grocers'-hall-court, Poultry. John Eiger, jun, was in my employ—I had seen his father at my place twice—I had about one hundred carpet bags—on the 3rd of Oct. I missed some—I spoke to John Elger, jun., about them—he denied all knowledge of them—I went to his father's house on the 4th of Oct., and said to him, "This is a
pretty mess John has got into about the carpet bags"—he came down and asked his son if he knew anything about them—he said he did sot—Elger, the father, said that he could lay down his life and his existence that his son was innocent—I have found forty-seven of them—these four are mine.
STEPHEN WHITTAKER . I am a pawnbroker. These four carpet bags were pawned on the 23rd Sept., by John Elger, sen.
WILLIAM MORRELL ( police-constabe G 60.) I went to the house—both the prisoners were there—I took them to the station, and charged them with the felony—on the way back to the house, the father said, "It is very hard to accuse an innocent man, I have never seen them"—I was looking about for the duplicates, and the son said, "You need not look, you will find no tioket of the bags, I can tell you"—I did not find any.
John Elger, sen. I have a wife and nine children—I have been for the last eighteen months in utter distress.
JOHN ELGER, jun.— GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
JOHN ELGER — GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Nine Months.
(There were four other indictments against the prisoners.)

3066. WILLIAM JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of Oct., 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Poulton, from his person.
WILLIAM WEST ( police-constable T 106.) I was in the hall of the New Court, Old Bailey, about five in the afternoon of the 24th of Oct., waiting to go before the Grand Jury—I watched the prisoner, and saw him take something from a gentleman's pocket, and put it under his jacket—I took this handkerchief from under his jacket—I found on him these two other handkerchiefs, and these two knives.
THOMAS POULTON . I am a soda-water manufacturer. I missed my handkerchief, and just at that moment West showed it to me—this is it.
Prisoner's Defence. I found it at my feet; I was going out and West took me; I did not know whose it was.
GUILTY .* Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3067]
3067. MARY CARROLL was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of Oct., 2 gowns, value 1 l. 18 s.; 3 petticoats, 7 s. 6 d.; 1 shawl, 15 s.; 1 pair of stays, 14 s. 6 d.; 1 bonnet, 14 s. 6 d.; 1 pair of boots, 4 s. 6 d.; 1 collar, 3 s. 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief, 3 s.; the goods of Elizabeth Daley.
ELIZABETH DALEY . I am an unfortunate girl, and lodged with the prisoner's mother—on the 6th of Oct. I went to bed, leaving my things all safe—when I got up they were all gone, and the prisoner too—she slept with me—these are all my things—( looking at them.)
ELIZABETH SPREADBURY . I produce the boots, petticoats, and bonnet—I found the prisoner wearing them on the 7th of Oct.

MARY ANN LAWS . I am single. On the 23rd of Oct. I was in Pitfield-street, and saw the prisoner take a pair of shoes in her right hand, and cut them from the prosecutor's door—I went and told the young man, and she was taken back—I saw her give them to another person, who crossed the road and was gone.
GEORGE BURNELL . I am shopman to Hiram Crisp—some shoes hung by the side of the door on the 23rd of Oct.—I missed a pair—I had seen them safe about an hour before.

[trialAccount t18431023-3069]
3069. HENRY JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of Oct., 2 pewter pots, 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of William Coomber; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM COOMBER . I am a licensed victualler, and live in Dorset-street I swear these two pewter pots are mine—the prisoner had no right to take them.
JOHN HADDOCK . I was going down Harley-street last Wednesday evening—I saw the prisoner stoop down an area and take a quart pot—he put it into his pocket—he went into the Park, took it out, took a knife out, stooped, and appeared to be cutting it—he then returned, and took two pint pots from an area—I gave information to an officer.
Prisoner. Q. What are you? A. A labourer—I have worked for Mr. Grant, a coachmaker in Hatton-garden—I have been convicted of potstealing myself.
WILLIAM JONES ( police-constable D 59.) I received information, and took the prisoner—I found on him these three quart pots and these two pints.
Prisoner. Haddock was the person who induced me to do anything of the kind; and he was on this very occasion after the same property; if I had not taken it he would.
JOHN SPIERS ( police-constable D 142.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18431023-3070]
3070. MARY RYAN was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of Oct., 1 box, value 3 s.; 12 yards of lace, value 1 s.; 3 collars, 3 s.; 6 ostrich feathers, 1 s.; and 1 tea-caddy, 2 s.; the goods of Mina Wood.
MINA WOOD . I am single, and live in Queen's-place, Great Queen-street; the prisoner was employed by me. On the 14th of Oct. I found her outside my door—I looked at her very hard—she said, "Don't look at me; I have got nothing belonging to you"—I said, "I did not say that you had"—I sent my servant out for an errand—she came back and said did I give the woman two boxes—I went after the prisoner, and found her with two boxes under her apron—I asked her what she had got, she said, "Nothing"—these are them—she had no right to them—I had placed them under my bed.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. What was she employed as? A. A charwoman—she had got about eleven yards from my door—I keep a lodging-house—I do not inquire what my lodgers' friends are—I do not know what my lodgers do—mine is not a house of ill-fame.
JURY. Q. Who admitted her into the house? A. There is a latch and a handle, which must be turned—I am quite sure my door was shut
THOMAS TICKNER ( police-constable F 11.) I took the prisoner at the prosecutrix's house.
Cross-examined. Q. The boxes were in the house? A. Yes, all the prisoner said was, "You are quite mistaken"—I know nothing about the house, it is not on my beat.
MARY HEIFY . I went out on an errand, and met the prisoner coming out with two boxes—I asked her if she had seen my mistress—she said, "Yes, it is all right"—I had cleared the room before, and saw the box under the bed.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3071]
3071. GEORGE RICHARDS, alias Ritchie , and WILLIAM CHRISTIE were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of Oct., at St. Marylebone, 12 forks, value 8 l.; and 12 spoons, 12 l.; the goods of Thomas Todd Mardon, the master of Christie, in his dwelling-house; and that Richards had been before convicted of felony.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY BROOK . I am footman, in the service of Mr. Thomas Todd Mardon, of Wirapole-street, Cavendish-square—Christie has been butler then for ten years—he had the care of the plate—it had been put away for some time—in August last it was taken out, counted, found right, and given again into the care of Christie—he had the key of the boxes—Richards has been in the habit of coming to my master's, to see Christie, and came sometimes when we had parties, with my master's knowledge. On the 6th of Oct., about half-past eleven, or a quarter to twelve o'clock in the day, as I went out on an errand, I passed the butler's pantry—Richards and Christie were in it—in about a quarter of an hour I returned, and met Richards at the corner of Wimpole-street, coming in a direction from the house—I went in, and saw Christie there—he went out soon after, and did not return any more—the next day a policeman came—the plate chest was opened, and a dozen table-spoons, and two dozen table-forks, were missed from it—in looking about afterwards, I found some duplicates—I gave them to the policeman—these are my master's spoons and forks, and have the family crest on them.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You have been some time in the service? A. Eighteen months—I never took anything out of pawn daring that time—I had forgotten, I did take a pair of my master's boots out—I did not give them to Richards—he did not tend me for them—that was between three and four months ago—the butler said it was my duty to go.
Cross-examined by MR. JONES. Q. Was your master in the habit during the summer-time of going to the sea-side? A. He did not this year—he did last, and during that time the whole of the plate was left in Christie's care—it is worth a great deal.
JOHN BOOKER . I manage the business of Mr. Neal, a pawnbroker, in Duke-street, Manchester-square. On the 6th of Oct. Richards came to the shop, a little after twelve o'clock—he brought twelve table-spoons and twelve forks, and wanted to pledge them for 16 l.—I questioned him—he said they were his own, and his own crest was on them—after that he said it was his father's crest—I looked at it, and said I should go with him—we went out together—he said he lived in Queen-street—when we came out, he went towards Manchester-square—he did not go by a direct road—he took me to a public-house, and wanted me to go into the parlour—I declined it—at last we got to High-street, Marylebone, and then to Wimpole-street—we met a person coming up the prosecutor's area steps—he asked some questions, and then we went on to the end of the street, and saw a policeman, who took him.
JOSEPH NIFTON ( police-constable D 118.) I was in Mr. Neal's shop this day—I followed Booker and the prisoner when they came out—after following them up and down the streets, I took Richards, and took him to the station—I went to Mr. Mardon's house in Wimpole-street next day—the box was opened, and this plate was missing.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you search Richards? A. Yes—I found on him a little money.
JOHN STANLEY ( police-constable D 48.) I was at Marylebone police station when Christie came there, on Wednesday, the 25th of October—he said, "My name is Christie"—I said, "You come from Wimpole-street?"—he said, "Yes, my attorney will be here at one o'clock"—he said, "I come to surrender myself for the concern in Wimpole-street."
JAMES FELL . I am clerk at the police court. I was present when Christie was examined—he made a statement, which was read over to him—this is it—( read)—"I wish to speak the truth: I have lived at Mr. Mardon's the last ten years, and found him a very good, kind matter—up to last August I
always conducted myself in a proper way, and never thought of doing anything wrong, until last August, when I engaged George Richards to assist me in cleaning the furniture; while up stairs with him, I was called by the housemaid to show me that the wardrobe doors had burst open; I then looked into it, and saw a small portmanteau and a bunch of keys; I unfortunately opened it, before the housemaid and Richards; after that I shut the wardrobe, and went down to clean the furniture, and while with Richards, cleaning the furniture, we commenced talking to each other; but previous to that, I told him I was in arrears of rent, and several other little bills, and he told me I need not be in want for no money, for he would go and pledge these things for me; so, in the course of two or three days after, I unfortunately let him go with part of the plate, and then, some days after that, we went with some more of it; when that plate that belonged to the portmanteau was pledged, I began to feel very uncomfortable and uneasy in my own mind, and I told Richards so; then he advised me take some of the plate that was under my care, to take out what belonged to the portmanteau, and I did so, with the intent of taking them out again; I have given myself up, with the expectation of Mr. Mardon and the Court showing me mercy."
(Christie received a good character.)
CHARLES FOX . I produce a certificate of Richards's former conviction which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person—I was not present at his trial—I was in Court when sentence was passed against him.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Was he sentenced by himself or with others? A. With ten or a dozen others—it was for a robbery at the Travellers' Club, in which service I was—I think the late Recorder sentenced him—I saw him going from the bar, and he shook his head at me.
CHRISTIE — GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.
Confined One Year.
RICHARDS — GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3072]
3072. GEORGE HENRY HAISELL was indicted for forging and uttering, on the 20th of Feb., a certain bill of exchange for the payment of the sum of 37 l. 12 s., with intent to defraud Elizabeth Hatton.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and CARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS CHARLES SIRRELL . I am a gold-refiner, and live in Barbican. The prisoner frequently called on me—I could not undertake to say he brought this bill with him, as he was in the habit of coming himself, and of sending a young man who is since dead—I discounted this bill ( looking at it) a day or two after it was drawn—I could not undertake to say whether I paid the money to the prisoner, or to the person be sent the bill by, but it was on the faith of his note from the firm, on the faith that he had authority from the firm to draw it—if the prisoner did not bring it, a person named Woolley did—this is my signature to these depositions—( looking at them)—this bill, marked No. 1, is for 37 l. 12 s.—it purports to be drawn by procuration of Elizabeth Hatton and Co.—I discounted it—I do not remember the day, except by the bill-book—I would not undertake to say that the prisoner brought me this bill—he brought me one bill, and two were brought to me by Woolley, to the best of my recollection and belief—I never said that to the best of my belief it was the prisoner—I could not say whether it was No. 1, 2, or 3, he brought.
ELIZABETH HATTON . I carry on the business of a chemist and Prussianblue maker, in Elder-street, Spitalfields—it was formerly the business of my son—he has been dead three years. In Feb. last the prisoner was in my service, at a salary of 150 l. a-year—he had no authority from me to draw bills
by procuration in my name—he was not in any way interested in the affairs of my business, except by payment of his salary—I did not give him authority in Feb., or at any time, to draw this bill, or any other, by procuration—I first knew there was such a bill five or six weeks ago from the gentleman who held it—I have never received any proceeds for this bill, either in money or in money's worth.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. I believe your concern sustained considerable loss just before your son's death? A. It might—I never was informed of a loss of 1200 l.—it never was suggested together—I have occasionally drawn on my customers for their accounts, I cannot say how many times since my son's death, but then I endorsed them—my name went to them—the prisoner presented the bills to me, and came for my acceptance, for me to put "E. Hatton and Co." on them—I never authorized him to draw a bill by procuration, nor ever authorised it to be done, I am quite sure—he had the management of this concern—I lived at a distance, but occasionally came there on business—the money was generally sent to me at the end of the week by some one from the factory, or the prisoner brought it himself—the endorsement, "E. Hatton," on this bill—( looking at one)—is in my handwriting—this other endorsement is also in my writing—this other one is also, and so is this one—( these were all drawn by procuration by the prisoner)—this cheque on my bankers is in my handwriting—this signature, "E. Hatton," to this letter, is in my handwriting.
Q. Did the prisoner ever draw bills in his own name, for the purpose of carrying on the concern; I mean bills drawn by himself, without mentioning your name at all, in order that he might raise money by discounting these bills? A. Not till lately—he has done so lately, as I have been given to understand—this is my acceptance to this bill drawn by the prisoner in his own name on the firm—this other one is mine—I did not give him unlimited authority to raise money in any way he thought best, for the purpose of carrying on the business, not without my knowledge—I have no recollection of having sworn before the Magistrate, that, except in one instance where I accepted a bill drawn by the prisoner for 50 l., which was for his own salary, I never accepted a bill in my life drawn by him—I never knew of any others but what my hand went to—I never told the Magistrate that I never accepted a bill drawn by the prisoner, except that one for 50 l. for his salary—I acknowledged there were several other bills, but not by procuration—I never accepted any bill drawn by the prisoner in his own name without I put my hand to it. Q. I wish you would give me an answer; I want to understand what it is you are prepared to swear; do you mean to deny that you told the Magistrate that you had never accepted a bill drawn by the prisoner oh the firm, except the one for 50 l. for salary? A. I certainly did not say it; not by procuration, the bills are so different—I do not know anything of other bills.
Q. I am speaking of bills drawn by your own clerk, and accepted by you, to get money to carry on the business by? A. I acknowledge there were three bills of this kind before the Magistrate, of Mr. Sirrell's—they were the first that I saw.
Q. Do you mean to say now, on your oath, that those that I have shown you to-day, which have your own handwriting on them, were not seen by you before? A. Of course they were.
Q. What did you mean by saying, till you saw these three, you had no idea that he had ever drawn a bill by procuration on your house? A. I never was aware of it.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you ever authorize, or were you ever aware that
the prisoner had drawn, accepted, or issued any bill of exchange for any purpose, except that to which, as drawer, indorser, or acceptor, you had put your name? A. No.
Q. Look at this bill, which is drawn "Value received. P. P. E. Hatton and Co. G. H. Haisell." Look at the signature to the bill? A. This "Hatton and Company" is not mine—I see "P. P. E. Hatton and Company. G. H. Haisell—I do not know what this "P. P." means.
( MR. CLARKSON here withdrew from the prosecution.)
(There were three other indictments, on which no evidence was offered.)
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18431023-3074]
3074. SAMUEL FULLBROOK was indicted for a misdemeanor.
PETER HAYNES . I live in Oxford-street, Commercial-road. I am the father of William Haynes, who is committed for the murder of his wife—the Jury sat on her body—the prisoner came to my house, and wished to see the body, because, he said, he was one of the Jury—I let him do so—he then asked me for some vinegar to wash his hands, and asked when I intended to bury it—I said the next day—he said, "Where?"—I said, "At Haggerston church"—he said, "I am the parish clerk"—I said, "Perhaps you will be so kind as to take the warrant for interment, and see to the grave"—he said he would, and said, "There are dues fot the interment"—I said, "Very well, what are they?"—he said, "18 s. 6 d.," which I gave him for the dues—when I began to reflect in the evening, I thought I had been deceived, and waited on the person who I found to be the parish clerk the next morning—I should not have given the prisoner that 18 s. 6 d. if he had not told me he was the clerk—if I had not believed it I should not have given it him—I did not perceive that he was drunk.
SAMUEL ROUSE . I am parish clerk of St. Mary, Haggerston; I have been there four years. The prisoner has never acted as parish clerk while I have been there—he had no authority to take any money for burials or anything.
Prisoner. I had been drinking in the morning.
GUILTY . Aged 48.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3075]

3076. WILLIAM COOPER was indicted for unlawfully breaking and entering the shop of William Shrub, with intent to steal.
WILLIAM SHRUB ( police-constable D 49.) About ten minutes past three o'clock in the morning of the 4th of Oct. I heard a smash of glass—I looked, and saw the prisoner standing with his back to me, and his arm through a pane of glass that was broken in the shop window—he pulled out his arm, and ran off—I pursued—he was secured without ray losing sight of him—I swear his arm was through the window.
CHARLES HALL . I am a watchmaker. I left my shop, in the Edgware-road, safe on the 3rd of Oct., fastened with a bar and four shutters—I came the next morning, a shutter was then down, and the glass was broken—there were watches, clocks, and other property in the shop.
JAMES CREEK ( police-constable D 94.) I stopped the prisoner—I found these four keys in his pocket.
Prisoner's Defence. Does it stand feasible that I could take a shutter down from a strong iron bar?
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3077]
3077. MARIA BROWN was indicted for obtaining money under false pretences.
WILLIAM THOMAS LYON . The prisoner was in my service two years—she had to pay several sums, such as milk, bread, and little things for the house—these are the papers of them—they are merely memorandums, and have no dates, because I considered they were done with; but I found that bills were brought in by my baker and milk-man to the amount of some pounds, when I did not consider that I owed any thing—on the 19th of May, I paid her 1 s. 4 1/4 d. for the baker, Richard Gullins—I am sure she came to me for that sum for him, and I paid it—I also paid her 6 d. for milk for Richard Stukeley, the told me she wanted the money for him, and I gave it her—she brought me the bills of them, as an account of the money she had expended.
RICHARD GULLINS . I did not receive of the prisoner 1 s. 4 1/4 d. on the 19th of May—the money is still due.
THOMAS BYTHAN . I live with Mr. Stukeley—I did not receive anything of the prisoner on the 19th of May—the money is still due.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18431023-3078]
3078. GEORGE CHAPMAN and MATTHEW HODGSON were indicted for obtaining money by false pretences.
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18431023-3079]
3079. JAMES PENDLETON and OLIVER MARTIN were indicted for stealing, on the 12th Oct., at St. George, Bloomsbury, 2 watches, value 7 l. 14 s.; 1 guard-chain, 16 s.; 2 spoons, 1 l. 10 s.; 2 sovereigns, 7 half-sovereigns, 1 crown, 10 half-crowns, 40 shillings, 30 sixpences, and 60 groats; the property of James Smith, in his dwelling-house.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
SOPHIA SMITH . I am the wife of James Smith, publican, Kinggate-street, Holborn—on the 12th of October I went into my bed-room, which is the front room, second floor, and put two sovereigns, a crown piece, two half-crowns, and seven shillings, into a drawer—there were two spoons and a watch of my husband's in it—I locked it—a tea-caddy was standing on the drawers—my watch was in the caddy—I left the room between twelve and one in the day, locked the door, and hung the key on a nail in the bar parlour—the prisoners had been customers—as I was coming down stairs, I saw the prisoners and another man not in custody, in the bagatelle-room, which is on the first floor—they were standing by the board—one had the mace in his hand, and the other was sitting down—I had seen the third man there before in the prisoners' company two or three times—about half-past one the prisoners came from up stairs, and passed through the bar parlour, the bar, and the passage, and went out—they asked for some gin—I was at dinner, and my husband served them—they were all three together—they paid their reckoning and went away—I dined in the bar parlour, which commands a view of the bar—no one had been up after I came down—about four I went into my bed-room and found the drawer and tea-caddy broken open, and the money, watches, and spoons gone—the lock of the door was picked, but not injured in the least—it could not have been opened by my key, because I had it down stairs—there is no other way of going into the bagatelle-room, and to my bed-room, but through the bar parlour—I can undertake to say, that no one had
gone up those stairs from the time I came down and hung up the key, till my husband went up after the prisoners had gone—I had no servant—I have two lodgers up stairs, but they went out at nine o'clock, and did not return till the evening.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. What are your lodgers? A. One has been a publican—he had only left his business about a week, and was looking out for another; the other one is in some trade, I do not know what—the house contains eleven rooms—it is not sometimes frequented by sporting characters—we have card-playing sometimes—I do not remember my husband losing at cards a few days before this, and having to go up stairs to get money to pay for it; if he had, he would have asked me, because I had the key of the drawer where the money was—I cannot say whether I had it two days before—two of our rooms are unoccupied—one is a bagatelle-room, one a spirit room, one a kitchen, and the others spare rooms—if a person came for a lodging he would have had one of these rooms—one of them was occupied a week before, the other was not—our house is pretty well attended of an evening sometimes—I do not recollect any card-playing in our house an evening or two before—I do not know two men named Mason and Crook—I might know the men—I do not remember a man they call the Brewer's Collector playing at cards—I was there when he came in on Wednesday evening, and he did not stay more than twenty minutes—I think there were one or two playing at cards—I did not hear a bet of ten crowns to one—I do not know whether they were playing for money—there is a water-closet just as you get outside the parlour door, adjoining the stairs.
MR. DOANE. Q. Did you see the men go away that were playing at cards? A. Yes—I am sure they left the house, and the collector too—that was the day before this.
JAMES SMITH . I keep this public-house. Between twelve and one o'clock this day, I saw the prisoners and another man at our house—I had seen them in company several times before—Pendleton and the other man came in company on this day—Martin came in soon after, and asked if his friends were there—he walked up stairs into the bagatelle room—my wife was up stairs in the bed room—before Martin came in Pendleton ordered a glass of gin and water—I went up stairs, and saw him and the other man there when I took it up—I came down stairs, and in the course of a minute Martin came in—I afterwards saw them altogether up stairs in the bagatelle room—I was not absent from the bar more than a minute, when I took up the gin and water—while I was gone I let the flap of my bar down, and shut the door, so that no one could have gone up stairs while I was there without my meeting them—no one was up stairs but my wife to my knowledge—she came down in ten minutes, or a quarter of an hour—she came into the bar where I was—I did not leave the bar, except when I went up with the gin and water, unless my wife was there—I remember the prisoner and the third man coming down stairs—they had something to drink at the bar, paid for what they had up stairs, and left—while we were at dinner, no one came in to go up stairs, till the prisoners went away—if they had I must have seen them, as they must have passed through the bar and the bar parlour—no one went out after the prisoners—I went up stairs about four o'clock, with the key of the bed-room, which I took from the bar parlour—I found the lock had been picked—the lock had not gone quite back—the drawer had been rifled, and the property in the tea-caddy was gone—the money that was gone was about 10 l.—on the Saturday night after, I was in Fleet-street, and saw the prisoners with several others—I ran to the officers who were before me, and Restieaux took Pendleton—Martin started off,
ran through Fleet-street, and through the Temple—he then fell down, and was taken.
Cross-examined. Q. Don't you know that a night or two before Martin had given a man a good thrashing, and he had got a summons out for him? A. No—I was close to him when he was taken—the officer said, "I want you for a robbery in Kingsgate-street"—I did not hear Martin say, "On, that is it, I was just going up there for a box of cigars on which I paid 1 s. 6 d."—he had paid 1 s. 6 d. there—I might have played at cards a night or two before the robbery—I had to go up stairs for money to pay my losings, but not with the prisoners, they were persons who have used the house these four years—respectable men—I cannot say what I lost.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX ( police-constable E 49.) On the 14th of Oct. I was with the prosecutor, and his brother in Fleet-street—I saw the prisoners—I secured Pendleton—he said he knew nothing about it, but he had played at bagatelle there with a friend of his.
JOHN JAMES ALLEN ( police-constable E 159.) I was with the prosecutor in Fleet-street—I saw Mason run away—I followed him through the Temple—he fell down, and I secured him.
NOT GUILTY .

3082. REBECCA ARCHER was indicted for bigamy.
WILLIAM DREDGE . I married the prisoner at Wellington, about twelve years ago last May—I have a certificate of the marriage.
RALPH SIMPSON . I am a butcher, and live at Horncburch. In 1824, I saw the prisoner married to John Archer, who is in Court—she had sworn herself with child by him—I was a constable at the time.
JOHN CLAPSON ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner at Barking.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not know my first husband was alive, as he had left me seventeen years; I was informed he was dead two years after he left me.
WILLIAM DREDGE re-examined. I heard nothing of Archer till two or three months ago, and did not know whether he was alive or dead.

[trialAccount t18431023-3083]
3083. GEORGE SLENNET was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying William Beech.
WILLIAM BEECH . I am a shoemaker, and live in Angel-lane, Stratford. The deceased William Beech was my son—on the 16th of Aug., near eight o'clock in the evening, I was in the marshes, near Temple mills, and saw the prisoner second Charles Gilson, in fighting with my son—I do not know how it began—they had had a fight the day before, and also that morning—when
I first saw them in the evening, they were going down the marshes, and a great many people with them, to see them fight—I followed—I tried to prevent their fighting in the morning, and so I did in the evening, but could not—my son was past my jurisdiction—he told me if he did not fight, he should be called a coward—he was turned twenty years of age—I saw them fighting, and saw my son get weak—Slennet said to Gilson, "Now is your time; give it him; follow him up"—after that, Gilson hit him on the side of the head, knocked him down, and fell on him—my son became stupid, and did not speak any more—he only groaned—he died at half-past eleven o'clock at night.
Prisoner. Q. Did not he say, after he fell, "You have beat me to-night, and the next time I meet you, I shall serve you the same as you have served me to-night." A. I did not hear him—I did not go into the ring and back my son—I kept along with the people, alongside where they were fighting—I did not say, "My boy shan't be beaten by a boy like that."
JOHN BAILEY . I am a cordwainer. I saw the fight in the morning, but not in the evening—I saw nothing of the prisoner.
CHARLES HOWELL . I am a surgeon, and live at Stratford. I saw the deceased about half-past ten o'clock on the 16th of Aug.—he was quite insensible, and breathing with great difficulty—I bled him, but it did not relieve him—he died about three quarters of an hour after—his death was caused by extravasated blood on the brain—I opened the head thirty-six hours after the death—there was a blow externally on the left side of the head—it seemed a violent blow, and in my judgment was the cause of his death.
JAMES DYSON . I saw the fight, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening. The prisoner was taking part in it; at least I think it was him; but there are two brothers, so alike, that I could not swear it was him—the parties were quite agreeable to fight, and there was nothing unfair in it—it did not last longer than ten or fifteen minutes—I think the injury the deceased received was principally through falls—there were not many very severe blows—he had a very severe fall or two backwards, in the struggle, and he fell undermost.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not go with any intention to second the young man. His brother picked him up three rounds; he then took his clothes, and said to me, "George, I wish you would have the kindness to pick my brother up:" I said I would, and did so.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18431023-3084]

3087. CHARLES CROMBIE was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of Aug., 2 50 l. Bank-notes; the property of Elizabeth Lavallin, in the dwelling-house of James Cucksey.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH LAVALLIN . I am a widow, and live in Upper George-street, Greenwich. The prisoner lodged there wfth me and my daughter, whom he married—I went to London, and received the portion of some property which came to me—I had two 50 l. and three 5 l. Bank-notes—I returned—he knew I had received the money—I did not tell him where I put it—I put it in a bag, into a closet, and saw it safe about nine o'clock in the morning of the 16th of Aug.—the key was left in the lock of the closet-door—I went to the closet about six o'clock, and missed the money—the prisoner had left then—he said a person named Leicester had sent to him to attend a funeral at Deptford; he was to be there at half-past two o'clock, and should return in time to take the baby to her mother, who war nursing at a gentleman's house—he never returned—my son Henry bad taken the numbers of the notes, and he gave them to the police-inspector—I saw my son take the numbers.
Prisoner. You had no home, and were with me twelve or eighteen months, and only gave roe 3 l. or 4 l. the whole time. Witness. I deny it—I took cars of the child, and my daughter Louisa worked at her sister's business—I have paid you 10 l. for rent.
HENRY LAVALLIN . I live at Mr. Ender's rope-manufactory, Greenwich. On the 15th I saw my mother receive the money—I took the numbers of the notes as soon as we returned home—I gave her the paper.
EDWARD FREEMAN . I am inspector of the Greenwich police. On the 16th of Aug. I received information of this robbery, and the numbers of the notes—the two 50 l. were 07924 and 07925, the 5 l., 78662, 63, and 64.
THOMAS WHITMORE . I am a clerk in the Bank of England. On the 16th of Aug. a person came to the Bank, and received payment of this 50 l. note, No. 07924—the name of Lavallin is written on it.
MRS. LAVALLIN. I believe this name to be the prisoner's handwriting.
JOHN HAWKES . I am pay-clerk in the Bank of England. This 50 l. note, No. 07924, was paid on the 16th of Aug., by a person giving the name of Lavallin—I gave five 10 l. notes for it—two of them were Not. 55267 and 55269, dated 10th June, 1843.
ALEXANDER ROBERTSON . I am pay-clerk at the Bank. On the 16th of Aug., between two and four o'clock, I paid a person fifty sovereigns for this 50 l. note, No. 07925.
EDWARD PITT YOUELL . I am clerk at a banking-house at Yarmouth. On the 25th of Aug. the prisoner deposited 65 l. at our bank, in the name of Arthur Wensley, as I understood him—I received from him two 10 l. notes, Nos. 55267 and 69, dated 10th June, 1848, and two 5 l. notes, Nos. 78662 and 64, the rest in gold—he took 10 l. out on the 26th, and the rest on the 29th.
CHARLES STURGE FLETCHER . I am inspector of police at Cambridge. On the 26th of Oct. I found the prisoner at a house at Cambridge, and told him I took him on suspicion of stealing Bank notes from Mrs. Lavallin, of Greenwich—he said he knew nothing of Greenwich, and I was deceived; he was not the person, his name was Arthur Wellesley, and he came from London—I found a box and bag there—I asked if they were his—he said, "Yes"-—I found bills of parcels amounting to 46 l., and a gold watch in it—I asked him to write his name—he wrote "Arthur Wellesley, K. G. C., London," and said he was the son of the Duke of Wellington; and while in custody he wrote a letter to the Duke, begging His Grace to write a letter, that he might be released.
Prisoner's Defence. I did not know what I was doing; I had entirely lost my senses for some hours.
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3088]
3088. THOMAS THORN was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of Dec., at Woolwich, 1 watch, value 5 l.; 1 guard, 1 s.; and 1 key, 6 d.; the good of William Daniels, his master, in his dwelling-house.
MARY ANN DANIELS . I am the wife of William Daniels, and keep a public-house at Woolwich. On the 26th of Sept. I put my gold watch into the glass drawer on the table—I missed it about half-past two o'clock the same afternoon—this is it—the prisoner was there, helping to wait.
Prisoner. I was not a servant in the house, I was discharged the day before. Witness. I am sure he was in the house that day—he cleaned the pots.
THOMAS MARSON . I am a pawnbroker. I produce the watch which I received in pledge of the prisoner on the 26th of Sept.
Prisoner. Q. What dress had I on? A. A white moleskin dress, but no jacket—it was between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—I swear you are the person.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw the watch, nor ever had it in my possession. There was a man drinking in the house that day, dressed very much like me; I was at Woolwich all the day.
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 34.— Recommended to mercy.
Confined Six Months.

3089. WILLIAM FOSTER was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Oct., 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., and 2 shillings, the property of John Tester.

3090. WILLIAM COCHRANE was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of Oct., 1 purse, value 1 d.; 3 sovereign, 17 shillings, and 2 sixpences, the property of George Brown, from his person.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE BROWN . I am master of the Harriet coal-brig, and live at Poole, in Dorsetshire—I formerly knew the prisoner at Gibraltar. On Tuesday afternoon, the 10th of Oct., I met him, and went with him to several houses—we went into the Nile tavern—while there I took but my purse which contained three sovereigns, eighteen shillings, and two sixpences, to pay for something—the prisoner could see me do that—I put my purse into my pocket again—the prisoner remained four or five minutes after that, and then went out—I was not sober—he had called for a pot of beer——he had drunk a part of it—three or four minutes after he was gone, some soldiers came in, and said the boat was ready—I said, "Call for a pot of beer, and I will pay for it "—I put my hand into my pocket, and the purse was gone.
Prisoner. Q. Were you not in company with some bad women, drinking? A. No, I was three or four hours in your company—I was not in company, or drinking with any woman—I did not see you in the boat, and bid you good night—I was not carried to the boat—I never went near the boat.
EDWARD RICHARDSON . I am mate of the Harriet. On the 10th of Oct. I was in the Nile tavern, and saw the prosecutor and prisoner there—the prosecutor took out his purse, and put it into his pocket again—there was gold and silver in it—I afterwards saw the prisoner with one arm over the prosecutor's neck, and the other on the table—he was sitting at the right hand side of the prosecutor, and his hand was towards the right pocket which the purse was in—the prisoner remained a very few minutes after, and had hardly got out of the door when the prosecutor missed his money.
Prisoner. Q. At the time you first saw me, was any one else in company with the prosecutor? A. Yes, two or three of the ship's company, and it was in a house where several girls were drinking, but that was not in the house where the robbery took place—as soon as I came into the house, you took him from there to another house, and when I found him again, you got him from me again, and took him to another house—the prosecutor and you were sitting by your two selves at the time of the robbery—no one but you were with him, from the time he put the purse into his pocket till he missed it.
CAROLINE WEST . I am the wife of John West, who keeps the Marquis of Granby, at Woolwich. About a week or fortnight ago the prisoner came into our house a little after ten o'clock at night, and left two sovereigns with me—he came a little after six next morning with a soldier and two girls, and I returned him the money.
ROBERT COOK . I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery, at Woolwich. On the 11th of Oct. I saw the prisoner in the guard-room, with a box nod some tobacco, and silver in it—he put it up the fire-place, and said there was tobacco, if any one wished to use it.
WILLIAM TWYMAN ( police-constable R 127.) I apprehended the prisoner. I examined this chimney, and found this tobacco-box and 11 s. in silver in it—I shook the tobacco, and a sovereign fell out from between it—the prisoner said, "I know nothing of it, it is not mine"—he said that directly I found the box, before I examined it.
( The prisoner put in a written Defence, stating that the prosecutor was in company with several bad women, and was very drunk; that he helped to carry him to his boat, and left him; and the money found in his possession was given him by his father.)
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 29.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Month.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

3096. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for a fraud.
BENJAMIN GAWTHORPE . I keep the Duke of York public-house at Deptford. On the 9th of Oct. the prisoner came, and said he was a contractor, under Grisell and Peto, for the extension of the railway, that he had got thirty-five men under him, and asked me to let him have beer for the men, and he would pay me weekly—I believed that, and let him have six gallons that evening—he took it to the work, as he said—his wife took some, and he took some—I went the next morning, and found him at work at a different place, and gave him in charge.
Prisoner. I was drunk. Witness. You was not drunk at all—I found out about eleven o'clock the next day that you was not Grisell and Peto's gangsman—I let six gallons of beer go after I found you did not work for them, but that was because you should not go away till I could get a policeman.
JOHN EMMERTON . I am in the employ of Grisell and Peto. I have no knowledge of the prisoner—he had not thirty-five men at work under him for them, nor was he employed himself.
Prisoner. We should have gathered the money on Saturday night, and have paid him; I did not say I was a contractor; I applied for a supply of beer for myself and others, and to settle for it on Saturday night.
GUILTY . Aged 31.— Confined Three Months.

3098. RICHARD TRENT, CAROLINE MALLABONE , and JAMES KINO , were indicted for feloniously assaulting James White, on the 11th of Oct., putting him ia fear, and taking from his person, and against hit will, 1 pocket-book, value 1 s.; 2 sovereigns, and 15 l. note; his property; and feloniously beating, striking, and using other personal violenoe to him.
JAMES WHITE . I am a carpenter, and live in Russell-row, Locks-fields. On Tuesday, the 11th of Oct., as I was coming up the Walworth-rood, about a quarter past one o'clock at night, going home, some parties came behind me, and gave me a push on the neck, which threw me down—as soon as I was down, Trent's hands were in my right hand trowsers' pocket, and across my breast—I cried out, "Keep your hands out of my pocket"—I had scarcely spoken the words before a policeman came up, and made a grab at Trent, who instantly ran away—the policeman pursued him, sprang his rattle, and he was stopped by a constable by the Elephant and Castle—I found that I had lost my pocket-book, a 5 l. note, and two sovereigns—the note was in the pocket-book, in my breast pocket, and the sovereigns in my right hand trowsers' pocket—I had felt the book safe about two minutes before—I only saw Trent—it happened dose by a gas-light.
Cross-examined by MR. CHARNOCK. Q. Has anything of this kind happened to you before? A. Yes—I have been robbed three times, some years ago—I was the worse for liquor, but was perfectly capable of taking care of myself—I had been out in the day on business, and had refreshment at two places—the last place was the Alfred's Head—I had not been there more than a quarter of an hour—I had felt my pocket-book safe two minutes before this occurred, by putting my hand outside my coat pocket, and about three minutes before that I doubled down the top part of the paper, because It was too long for the depth of the pocket, to keep it out of sight—I had four sovereigns in my left hand pocket, which were not taken—I did not fall down from being tipsy—I was pushed down from behind—I saw no one but Treat—he was not assisting to raise me up in consequence of the helpless state I was in—I swear he did not try to raise me up at all—I had been to the Alfred's Head with a man who sells mahogany—there might be some women there—I did not see any girls of the town—I was not treating or drinking with any—I saw the prisoners searched—nothing was found on them.
WILLIAM JENNINGS ( police-constable P 95.) On the 11th of Oct. I was on duty in the Walworth-road, coming out of Union-court, leading into the main road, and saw the prosecutor and prisoners pass along nearly in a line together—King stepped into Union-court—I asked what he did there—he said, "I have not done anything"—about a minute afterwards I heard a fall on the pavement just outside the court—I stepped out, and saw the prosecutor lying on the pavement, and Trent leaning over him, with his left hand over his breast, and his right hand was drawing from his right-hand trowsers' pocket—the prosecutor said, "You have robbed me of my pocket-book and a 5 l. note"—I made a reach at Trent, and also at the female prisoner, who was standing about a yard from the prosecutor—Trent started off—I followed him, crying, "Stop thief," and sprang my rattle—he was stopped by a policeman
of the L division, without my losing sight of him, at the corner of St. George's-road, by the Elephant and Castle, about 100 yards from where he started—I did not see what became of Mallabone.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not see the commencement of this transaction? A. No—the prosecutor was lying about two yards from the entrance of the court—when I first saw King he was in the court—I was coming out of the court when King turned in—I did not see King do anything—he was not five yards from the prosecutor—I went into the road on hearing the prosecutor fall down—he was in liquor—Trent was not laying hold of him to raise him up.
ALFRED WILLIAM WAKEFIELD ( police-constable L 117.) About one o'clock in the morning I saw the prosecutor pass by, and the three prisoners walking and talking together, about three or four yards behind him—I lost sight of them for about ten minutes—I afterwards heard a rattle, and saw Trent running from the Walworth-road towards St. George's-road, and Jennings after him—I slopped him, and took him into custody—I took Mallabone between eleven and twelve next day, and King between two and three in the afternoon—I searched them all, but found nothing on either of them.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18431023-3099]
3099. 3100. JAMES WALLACE was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of Oct., 4lbs. weight of tobacco, value 16 s., the goods of John Dale, his master.

On the 12th of Oct. he went to London-bridge wharf to load out various goods which came by the steam packets—I did not deliver this cask of tobacco to him, nor see it till it was afterwards at Fenning's-wharf; but here is the delivery-book to prove he had it—I cannot prove he had it—there was a vacant space in the cask at Fenning's-wharf—he had this delivery-book that day—my clerk, who attends the wharf, made the entry.

at Fenning's-wharf, out of any wagon—the Magistrate said there was no occasion—the clerk who made the entry in this book is not here—I cannot swear there is any deficiency, except from the book.
JOHN THOROWGOOD . I am a dealer in marine stores. On the 12th of Oct., about eight o'clock at night, the prisoner came to my shop with some tobaceo—I did not buy it—it was left there.
