3102. JAMES LUCAS and STEPHEN NOWLAND were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Richard West, on the 8th of Oct., and stealing therein, 1 counterpane, value 1 l.; and 1 pair of trowsers, 1 s.; his property.
MARIA WEST . I am the wife of, Richard West, of Grange-road, Bermondsey. On Sunday morning, the 8th of Oct., I went to bed at two o'clock—this counterpane was then in a basket in the back kitchen—the window and doors shut and fastened—the kitchen window had not been opened for years—I got up at half-past six next morning, found the window broken open, and hanging on one hinge, and the counterpane and trowsers gone—the trowsers were thrown over the wall—I only lost the counterpane.
WILLIAM WELLERD . I live in Grange-road. About twenty minutes to seven o'clock, on Sunday morning, I was passing the prosecutor's to go to my employ, and saw Nowland on the garden side of the wall, delivering the counterpane over to Lucas—he dropped it into his hands—Lucas tied it up, and put it on his head—I went to look for a constable, and lost sight of them—I knew them before, and swear to them—I returned to the house, and found a constable there—I described them—he went into the street, and took Nowland.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. What are you? A. A leather factor—I work for about half an hour on Sundays—I thought this was a robbery, and went for a constable.
WILLIAM FORDHAM ( policeman.) I observed the window of West's house about seven o'clock in the morning—it had been forced open by some instrument—I apprehended Nowland,
JOHN WRIGHT ( policeman.) I took Lucas, with the counterpane on his head, about five minutes to seven o'clock—Nowland was taken first, but they had separated.
LUCAS — GUILTY . Aged 18.
NOWLAND — GUILTY . Aged 18.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18431023-3103]
3103. THOMAS GRANT was indicted for burglariously breaking into the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Hackett, about twelve in the night of the 9th of April, at St. John Southwark, and stealing therein, 24 shirts, value 2 l. 8 s.; and 10 pairs of trowsers, 7 l., her property; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH HACKETT . I am single, and live in Thornton-street, Dockhead, in the parish of St. John, Horsley down. On Saturday night, the 10th of April, I went to bed at ten o'clock, leaving the shop quite safe—I awoke just before twelve, looked out of window, and saw the door, as I thought, safe—I saw two women standing on the side facing my shop—I heard a noise, but saw nothing, and went to bed again—I got up at seven, found the fanlight broken, and missed twenty-four shirts, and seven pairs of trowsers—I know these eleven shirts to be mine, and part of what I missed.
GEORGE GALE . I live in Brewer Vcourt, Horsleydown, and sell ginger-beer. I came out about four o'clock in the morning of the 10th of April, and observed the prisoner and four others come by my stand from towards the prosecutrix's shop—I am certain the prisoner was one—I asked if they wanted any coffee—they went on about twenty yards, stopped, and talked together—they then came towards my stand again, and one of them, who has since been convicted, took a cane off my stand—I told him to put it down—he would not—the prisoner came back and told him to put it down, or they should get blown, or some such word—they then went on to the bottom of the street into New-street—I saw them again at the end of a court facing the prosecutrix's shop—they staid there some time, as if talking to somebody, then went down the court again, and three of them came up the street—the prisoner was one—two females stopped down the street under the lamp—the men stopped by the prosecutrix's shop—two of them went down the court, and came back, then the prisoner went down and came back—the two men crossed the road, and went down the other side of the way, the prisoner kept on this side, and I lost sight of him.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You are not very certain "blown" was the word he used? A. No—they only passed me twice—they came up towards me three or four times—they were together—I saw the females down at the bottom of the street—that might be 200 yards from me—the three men were then together, close against the house—they were at one time all five together—both the other men were taller than the prisoner, and one was taller than the other—one of them spoke to me as they passed—I cannot say which—I saw the other two very soon after—the first time I saw the prisoner afterwards was before the Magistrate, on the 2nd of Oct.—I cannot say it was not the prisoner that spoke to me—I was at the corner of the street, just against the curb—it is a broad street—the five did not pass abreast, they were close together—two or three walked side by side—the men were before the women—they crossed over—their sides were to me—I saw their backs and front—I have sold coffee in the street about two years, and ginger-beer also—I am not able to do anything else.
THOMAS TURVEY ( police-constable N 196.) On the 10th of April, about nine o'clock in the morning, I was going on duty, and saw the prisoner, two other men, and two women, all in company together—I knew them all before—the two women had bundles in their aprons—I took the bundles from them—they contained the articles produced.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did you first see them? A. Crossing Redcross-street—I met them—three men walked in front, and the women behind them—the moment I stopped the women, the three men ran away—I went to get assistance, and followed them a considerable distance before I stopped them—I took the women, because they had the bundles—when I met them I
went about twenty yards, met my sergeant, and then went after them—I saw the prisoner in custody on the 30th of June and the 1st of July, and am positive he is the man—I had known him well for a considerable time.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read)—he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18431023-3104]
3104. JOHN WHITE BRIDGMAN and ISAAC BRIDGMAN were indicted for unlawfully breaking and entering the grave of Thomas Ghorst Tawney, and taking away the said body.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES DODSLEY TAWNET . I am the only surviving son of the late Thomas Ghorst Tawney, and shall be forty-seven years old in December. My father had a vault in the chapel of St. John's, West-street, Walworth—he died in Nov. 1803—I was not present at the funeral, but I recollect his death, and the funeral going from the house—at that time I had an elder brother living, named Thomas—he died on the 1st of Jan. 1837—I have no reason to believe that the tomb had ever been opened for the purpose of interments, between the death of my father and that of my brother, who was also buried in that vault—my mother died on the 27th Oct. 1837, and was buried in the same vault in Nov.—I attended the obsequies of my brother and mother—a person named Ashley opened and closed the tomb at both those interments—on both those occasions my father's coffin was entire, the lead part was—the wood, the external case, had decayed—it was wood cased with lead—it was safe and entire, as to the leaden coffin, both in Jan. and Nov. 1837—there was no other coffin interred in the vault in the meantime—I believe my father built the vault himself—I am a solicitor, and carry on business in King-street, Cheapside—I live in Regent-square—on Monday, the 11th of Sept. last, I received information, in consequence of which I went, accompanied by Ashley, to the house of the elder Mr. Bridgman, on the same afternoon—the door was opened by a servant, and when I inquired for Mr. Bridgman, she asked my name, which I gave—I saw Mrs. Bridgman—the public entrance to the chapel-yard is through the chapel—the coffins are taken that way into the burial-ground—there is no opening to go into the chapel-yard round the chapel—the only entrance is through the body of the chapel—Mr. Bridgman's house adjoins the chapel—Mr. Courtnall's is on one side, and Mr. Genet's on the other—I did not make known my business to Mrs. Bridgman—I sent my name in, and she said Mr. Bridgman would be there shortly—I saw Mr. Bridgman—I told him I came there for the purpose of opening my family vault, as I wished to see what it contained, and I had the bricklayers at the door for the purpose of opening it—he said nothing—I said, "I have the bricklayers here, I will let them in," and I walked into the passage, and opened the street-door, to let them in—it is a very small house—the parlour-door and street-door were close together—Mr. Bridgman said nothing to me—I walked towards the back part of the house, wishing to go through that way, from information I had obtained that there was a passage through the kitchen—Mrs. Bridgman met me at the door of the kitchen, and told me I could not go that way—I cannot say whether Mr. Bridgman was present—she showed me another way, through a little door by the front parlour-window, which led into the chapel, and from the chapel into the chapel-yard—I went through the chapel into the burial-ground, and went to the tomb of my late father, I saw that the large stone at the top had evidenly been removed, and the brick work damaged—the bricklayer's men removed the stone quite off the top of the tomb—I then observed that my mother's
coffin was turned on its side, on to my brother's, and my father's coffin was gone—that is the leaden coffin which I had observed safe in Nov. 1837—there were some pieces of wood lying at the bottom of the tomb, and a patch of hair about the size of my hand—that did not call my particular attention at that time—on the side of my mother's coffin, was a large patch of tallow, at if a candle had been stuck there and guttered—I desired to have my mother's coffin put on its bottom, that I might read the coffin plate on the top of it, and my brother's—the men had a great deal of difficulty in removing it, and talked of taking away the brick-work, but after looking between the lid of my mother's coffin they found this bed-winch jammed in between the lid of mother's coffin and the tomb, so as to make it fast—they could not move it—it has been in my possession ever since—after removing that, they put my mother's coffin flat on its bottom, on to my brother's coffin, not in the place where my father's had been—I directly went through the chapel into Mr. Bridgman's house, I should think that was not exceeding ten minutes after I first saw Mr. Bridgman—I saw the servant, but he was denied to me—I then required to see Mrs. Bridgman, and Mr. John Bridgman, but did not see them—in consequence of what the girl told me with reference to Mr. John Bridgman, I inquired for the hospital to which I supposed he was gone—I have since ascertained that he was at that time studying for the purpose of becoming a surgeon—I did not see him, or any of the family before I left the house that afternoon—after this, I consulted Mr. Clarkson on the subject, in consequence of which, on Saturday the 16th, I went to Union Hall, and applied for a warrant with reference to the younger defendant—I tbink no warrant was granted at that time—the elder defendant was summoned—he attended, and engaged to produce his son, who he represented to be away on the 27th—he did not appear on the 27th—on the 19th of Sept., I wrote a letter to Mr. Bridgman, and received an answer—in consequence of further information, on the 21st, I applied for a search warrant, and proceeded with it and Pritchard to the chapel-yard in question—I observed a spot which denoted that the earth had been recently removed—by my direction the earth was removed from that spot, and within five or six inches of the surface, a wooden coffin cased with lead was discovered—that was fifteen yards from the defendant's back premises—there was no lid to it—there appeared to have been one—the nail holes were perceptible at the side where the lid had been made fast—it was filled with loose earth, and some bones—the officers emptied it, and took it out, and it was left for some days in the chapel-yard in the custody of the police, day and night—after some days, the fragments of wood that I had seen in the tomb, on the 11th, were applied to the coffin, and it formed the entire wooden lid—it had been broken to pieces, and a hole knocked in the middle at the widest part, and when the whole was put together it fitted exactly, might have been nailed on through the old nail holes—I think the coffin was found on Wednesday the 21st, and it occurred to me on Saturday the 24th to do this—I made an appointment with the police on the Monday, to have the tomb fresh opened, and was obliged to have the bricklayers again to remove the stones—every possible search was made to find the lead belonging to the lid, but we could not find it—there was a little fir tree which appeared to have been recently planted a few feet from where the coffin was found—we searched there, and everywhere else where we saw any earth recently removed, but did not find the lead—we found bones, and skulls in different parts—close to the head of my father's coffin, we found three skulls, and in another spot quite close to the loose earth we found another skull—the part where the coffin was found, was in a little border to the left of a gravel walk that leads from the back of Mr. Bridgman's premises up to the stable—it was
not a place that appeared at all used for burial—in searching for the leaden lid, there was a tombstone raised on brick-work, which had evidently been pulled out—it was all loose—the bricklayers put their hands in, and pulled out three coffin plates—that was not my father's tomb.
COURT. Q. There was evidence that the ground had been disturbed for other purposes? A. Yes.
GEORGE GARFORD . I am a clerk in Messrs. Richards and Co.'s linen factory, in Bread-street, Cheapside, and am the son of Mrs. Courtnall; at the time in question I resided with her and my step-father, next door to Mr. Bridgman's, in West-street, Walworth. On Wednesday night, the 6th of Sept., I went to bed about eleven o'clock—I slept on the second floor, at the back of the house, which overlooks the chapel-yard in question—I put out my candle and drew up my blind, and on doing so, I fancied I saw something moving in the burial-ground—it immediately attracted my attention—I looked again, and in about five minutes' time I observed three men coming from the direction of the wall that separates the burial-ground from our garden, and go in the direction of Mr. Tawney's tomb—that was in the direction they would have come from Mr. Bridgman's house, without going through the chapel—I did not at that time know whose tomb it was—one of the persons appeared to have nothing on but a shirt, and a cloak over it—one of the others had a cloak, and the other, as far as I could see, was dressed in plain black clothes—I did not see the tomb opened—I observed that it was open—the stone that had been on it was removed to the side—I saw one of the men, I believe the undressed one with the cloak on, descend into the tomb, and saw something handed from the tomb—I also saw it carried away by two of them, one carrying it at each end—they appeared to carry it with great difficulty—I saw some ropes—I could not distinguish whether or not it was lifted up by those ropes—there was perhaps not sufficient light for me to discern whether what was taken out was a coffin, but I made the remark at the time that it was a coffin—it made that impression on me at the time—the third man then got out of the tomb—the other two carried the coffin in the direction of the wall from where I first saw them come—that is in a direct line with Mr. Bridgman's passage—after they got to a certain extent under the wall I could not see them, from the position of my window—it would lead them towards the house—(before I saw the coffin taken from the tomb, I had given an alarm to my father and mother)—the men afterwards returned from the wall where they appeared to have deposited what they took away, and all three returned to the tomb again—I then saw a lantern—it appeared to be brought from Mr. Bridgman's stable, which is in the burial-ground—one of them descended into the tomb with the lantern, the other two remained about the tomb—after the space of about ten minutes, I observed the party come out of the tomb again—I saw nothing more of the light after it was taken into the tomb—I saw a man bring a lever, or long wooden pole, from the direction of the stable, and replace the stone.
Q. Had you, in the course of this time, an opportunity of observing any one of the three persons, so as to enable you to speak to him? A. Only from his general appearance and action—I could not see his face, on account of the distance—I have known young Bridgman for a long time—I believe him to have been one of the three, the one that was undressed and had the cloak on—I could scarcely see whether he had shoes and stockings on—the wind blew about his cloak, when he had the lantern, which gave me an opportunity of seeing his person and appearance, and that assisted me in coming to the belief that it was young Bridgman—I had not seen him wear a cloak at any time that I remember.
COURT. Q. Then what do you mean by his appearance, if you could not see his face? A. From his height, manner, and actions; seeing him running backwards and forwards from the stable to the tomb—I had observed his style and gait before—I cannot say that there was anything peculiar about it—I was familiar with his mode of moving quickly.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. At that time had you any doubt that he was one of the three? A. No; on seeing this, I immediately went, hurried on what clothes I could, and went down stairs with the intention of going into the street, see whether any of the men went out that way—when I got into the passage, I heard something very heavy put down in Bridgman's passage—I made the remark at the time, that it was very heavy—I heard it distinctly, and called my father's attention to the fact—I proceeded to the outer door, opened it, and heard Mr. Bridgman's door open about the same time—I then observed a man come from the door of Mr. Bridgman's house—there is a wooden screen between the two houses, to prevent any one seeing the entrance in and out of the other—a man came from Mr. Bridgman's house, and went as far as the gate—the screen near the gate is not so high as it is near the door, and I saw him when he got near the gate—he looked over the screen, saw me, returned into the house again, and shut the door—I went into our house again, left the door ajar, and listened for a few minutes—in about five minutes Mr. Bridgman's door was opened again—I went to the door, and saw the man return again—that was certainly not young Bridg man—I heard the man say to the party who let him out, "Good night, ma'am "—(I had observed lights through the fan-light over the door, between the first and second times of the man's coming out)—he then went away, and walked towards Kennington—after he had walked about fifteen yards, he turned his head and saw me, as I had gone into the street, and then he ran as fast as he could—I ran after him for some time, but lost sight of him—he was the man that was dressed in plain black clothes—Mr. Bridgman kept a horse and chaise—whilst the men were at the tomb, I saw the horse come from the stable—I also saw a dog in the chapel-yard, while this was going on—I had seen it about the place before, it was quite quiet, it did not appear to treat the parties as strangers—I was not aware whose dog it was—I had to go to business early next morning—I returned about half-past seven in the evening, which was half an hour earlier than usual, for the purpose of following out this proceeding—I went to the same window from time to time, for two or three hours, to see if I could observe anybody; and a little before ten I saw two men at Mr. Tawney's tomb—they had a lever—(it appeared to me that the stone top of the tomb had been imperfectly put on the night before)—they used the lever in the same way as on the previous evening, to adjust the tomb-stone—they then came in the direction of Mr. Bridgman's house—young Bridgman was one of those men—I observed, when I saw him at the tomb, that he had a grey coloured coat on, cut in the shape of a shooting-jacket; and when they had completed what they were about at the tomb, and came in the direction of Mr. Bridgman's house, I went to the front of the house, looked through the window, and observed this party, dressed in the same way, come from Mr. Bridgman's house—I immediately ran down stairs, and followed him into the Walworth-road, without losing sight of him—I then passed him twice, and saw his face distinctly—I also heard him talking to somebody, and am sure it was young Bridgman—I then consulted my relatives, and thought it my duty to examine the tomb, to see to whom it belonged; and, on ascertaining, I examined the directory, to trace the relatives, which led me to Mr. Tawney's office, to whom I disclosed all I had seen.
JAMES COURTNALL . I married the mother of the last witness, and live next
door to the minister of West-street chapel, Walworth—my attention was called by my step-son to what took place on the night of the 6th—I cannot identify any of the parties.
ELIZABETH COURTNALL . I am the wife of last witness. On Wednesday night, the 6th of Sept., my attention was called to what took place in the chapel-yard next to our house—I saw three men engaged at the tomb—one of them was undressed, and had a cloak on—the other two, I think, were in black—two had cloaks on—I saw the dog in the ground, it was quiet—I saw the horse come out of the stable—on Monday, the 18th of Sept., from ten till one o'clock in the day, I was at our back window, and saw the elder defeudant at the wall of his ground, which separates our two houses—it was formerly a garden, but is now part of the chapel-yard—he seemed to be gardening, making a border—I am not sure whether I saw a spade—he bad no coat on—I cannot say for certain that he was digging, as my husband was gardening at the time, and it might have been his spade that I heard.
HANNAH COURTNALL . I am the daughter of Mr. Courtnall. About a fortnight before the 6th of Sept. last, I was walking in the garden, and heard Mr. Bridgman, senior, saying that some men had been seen in the burial-ground the night before—I do not know who he said it to—on the night of the 6th my attention was called to what was going on—I slept in the same room with Priscilla Terry, the servant—on the night alter I saw a man come out of Mr. Bridgman's house, and saw my brother follow him—shortly after, I was looking out of our second-floor window, which is even with Mr. Bridgman's second floor, and saw a female in her night-dress, looking out of the window—I am not able to say it was Mrs. Bridgman, but I think it was—at the same time I thought I saw a man with a hat on, looking out of the other window—I do not speak with any positiveness about that—that is their bedroom—I heard somebody talking in Mr. Bridgman's first floor, and heard some one say, "As long as my name is Bridgman"—it was a roan's voice—I thought I heard the voices of two men—I know the elder Mr. Bridgman's voice, and I thought it was his voice—I do not attend his chapel—on the following morning, about nine o'clock, or between nine and ten, I saw Mr. Bridgman's horse and chaise at the door; and between ten and twelve that same morning I saw Mr. Bridgman coming out of the chapel in West-street—he passed me—I saw his face distinctly—I was at home about ten o'clock in the evening, while my brother was watching, I saw the two men at the tomb, but could not distinguish young Bridgman—I saw my brother go out after one of them.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN. Q. The horse and chaise, I suppose, was one Mr. Bridgman, the elder, was in the habit of riding in? A. Yes—I was looking out of our first-floor front window when I saw the chaise—I saw it come, I did not see anybody in it—I beard it come to the door, and some one had just jumped out of it when I saw it—I heard them jump out—I had to go out twice that morning, and I do not remember whether it was the first or second time that I saw Mr. Bridgman—I passed him in the open street—I did not speak to him, nor he to me—I had to go about half a mile, and then I returned home.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Do you know whether Mr. Bridgman was in the habit of having his horse and chaise as early as that hour in the morning at his door? A. Yes.
PRISCILLA TERRY . I was in Mr. Courtnall's service. On Wednesday evening, 6th Sept., a little before seven o'clock, I was looking out of the window up stairs, and saw the elder Mr. Bridgman in his garden, coming in a direction from the stable to the back part of his house—a little time after, I was down
stairs, and heard a digging in Mr. Bridgman's garden—I slept in the same room with Miss Courtnall, and my attention was called to what was going on in the chapel-yard that night—I saw Mr. Garford follow one of the men next evening—after that I heard some two or three men's voices in Mr. Bridgman's house—one of the men said, "I will tell you what it is, John," and, "As sure as my name is Bridgman"—those two observations I heard at intervals, in the course of some conversation—I did not know the first voice—I thought the second voice was young Bridgman's—I have no belief as to the other voice—it was not familiar to me—I continued listening and looking out of window after that, and saw a woman look out of Mr. Bridgman's second-floor window—I thought it was Mrs. Bridgman—I also thought that I saw a man's hat at the other window of that bedroom—about eight o'clock next morning I heard young Bridgman's voice in the garden, calling "Father"—I heard no answer—he went down the garden, towards the stable, as if he was going to his father, as he did not hear him—I had not seen or heard the elder Mr. Bridgman that morning—I do not know exactly when I next saw him—I think I saw him next day in his garden, but I am not certain.
Cross-examined. Q. This way from the stable, that you speak of, comes along close by the wall, does it not? A. Yes—Mr. Bridgman is very often in his garden—it was from half-past six to seven o'clock on Wednesday, that I saw him—it was not dusk—I merely went into the room, looked out of the window, and came away directly.
EDWARD GENET . I am a clerk in the Custom-house, and live on one side of the chapel, and Mr. Bridgman on the other. I have a lodger who keeps a little dog—it is a very sharp little dog, which barks at strangers and persons coming in and out—I used sometimes to be bothered with its barking of a night, and have gone out to see what was going on—about a month before this transaction, I heard from the servant at No. 17, that some persons had been seen in the burial ground the night before—I mentioned the circumstance to Mr. Bridgman next evening, and his son was in the yard at the time—Mr. Bridgman made use of a scriptural quotation, and said it did not matter so much if the spirit was at rest, about the bones being disturbed; but if I thought there was any likelihood of anybody coming into the chapel-yard, he would have a watch set—I said I thought it might be a fancy of the lady next door—he said perhaps it was his servant-girl, that she walked in her sleep, and perhaps she walked into the chapel-yard—he said he had occasion, some years since, to attend at Union-hall about a transaction of that kind, which took place in his yard—I saw a fir-tree planted about the 11th or 12th, which was not there before, to my knowledge.
SARAH GENET . A lodger of ours had a dog, which used to disturb us by constantly barking—I heard from a lady at No. 17, that some persons had been seen in the burial-ground, and remember my husband stating that to Mr. Bridgman, who replied, that he did not think any one came after the bodies in the ground—he made some scriptural allusion, and said if the spirits were at rest, it did not so much signify about the bodies—I do not recollect the exact words, but he said if my husband thought so, he would have a watch in the yard—my husband said, no, he did not think it was required—Mr. Bridgman said he did not know that any persons had come there for a very long time, once there was a case of some persons being taken up, and he had to appear against them—about a fortnight or three weeks before this tomb was disturbed, young Bridgman borrowed the lodger's dog—he said his father's horse shyed at dogs, and he wanted to make it used to dogs—I think he had a little fancy dog of his own at that time—he returned the lodger's dog in about a week.
Q. Did the dog bark then in the same way it wed to do, or had it become much more quiet? A. If strangers came into the ground it barked, it would not bark at young Bridgman—the dog constantly ran in and out the burial-ground, and seemed acquainted with the whole of the family—I do not know whether it barked at them after this, it might occasionally—it did not sometimes—when young Bridgman returned it, the lodger offered to give it to him—he said he thought the horse was broken to the dog, and he had no further use for it, that they were going to dispose of the horse, to they would not wish it any further—on Thursday, the 21st of Sept., I was in my garden and saw Mr. Bridgman digging in his garden—I saw the spot where the coffin was afterwards found—it was not at that spot I saw him digging—it was on the same side, but nearer the stable—he was spreading the earth about with his spade—there was nothing hanging there at the time—I saw a counterpane or sheet hanging there the day before, but he was not there then—the coffin was found, I should say, three or four yards from where I saw him digging—it was in the same direction.
Cross-examined. Q. Was this on the 18th of Sept, which has been spoken of? A. I do not know the day of the month—it was on a Tuesday, I think, about eleven in the day—I never saw young Bridgman's dog—I heard him say he had one, but it would not bark, and therefore he wished to borrow one to accustom the horse to it—the fence dividing our premises from Mr. Bridgman's, has gaps in it—the palings have been broken down in places, which the dog could get through—he had been in the habit of going in and out of Mr. Bridgman's premises as he liked, ever since we have been in the house—it had the same opportunity of being familiar with Mr. Bridgman's family before it was borrowed as after.
Witnesses for the Defence.
MOSES REDDING . I am a baker, and live at No. 8, Staverton-row, Walworth-road. On the 6th of Sept. I remember seeing the elder Mr. Bridgman at Ramsgate, at the door of Cavendish chapel, about a quarter to nine o'clock, within a few minutes; I cannot say to a minute or two—I was with him for a quarter of an hour at least, in conversation.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. How came you at Ramsgate on the 6th of Sept.? A. I went down there on pleasure—I went on the 6th—there is no one here that went with me—I went by steam, arrived at Margate about a quarter to five o'clock, and went from there to Ramsgate by a buss —I believe the vessel I went by was the Herne, but I am not certain—I got to Ramsgate at half-past six—I did not go down in the same boat with Mr. Bridgman—I had no lodging there when I saw Mr. Bridgman at a quarter to nine, for I had just arrived there—I slept there afterwards—before I saw Mr. Bridgman I had been along with a part of my brother's family that was down at Margate, in George-place—I do not know that any of them are here—I went by myself—I am married—Cavendish-chapel is in Devonshire-street, near George-street or George-place—I never was there but once before, and am not certain of the name of the place or street—I know it was Cavendish-chapel, because my sister-in-law told me so—I believe it is a dissenting chapel, but do not know—I am a dissenter—I have not attended Mr. Bridgman's chapel for many years—my sister's family go to Cavendish-chapel occasionally—they had been staying down there five or six weeks—it was outside the chapel that I saw Mr. Bridgman, after service was over—I cannot say how long before I had seen him; it might be some days or weeks—I have known him for years—I am not in the habit of seeing him frequently—I do not visit at his house—I have not been there above once these four or five years—I was
speaking to him on this occasion about the sermon we had heard, and about his stopping down at Ramsgate—Mortlock Daniels preached that night—I have lived eighteen years in Staverton-place, and am a miller as well as a baker—I left my family in town while I went to Ramsgate, at least I left my home; but I left my wife where she has been for some time, she is out of her mind—I have one child—he is grown up.
Q. You understood from Mr. Bridgman's conversation that he had been to the chapel and heard the sermon? A. No; we met at the door—I cannot tell what it was we said about the sermon, but I know we were speaking about Mr. Daniels taking up the subject—I can tell you the text, or nearly so; it was the promise of Jacob about going down to Egypt, "I will go with thee into Egypt, and be sure to bring you back," or something of that sort, to the best of my recollection, I forget exactly, I am not certain of the chapter and verse; I think it was in Genesis, but I will not say positively—we conversed together for a quarter of an hour—nobody else was with us—we walked down the town towards where he was lodging, which, I think, was on some terrace, but I really do not know, I did not ask him—I did not see him on the following day—I went to Margate to meet ray friend, who I was to have met there on the Tuesday—I staid with my brother's family that night, and went away next morning—I came out of chapel with my brother's wife—I do not recollect that any one else of my brother's family was there—my brother's wife was at the chapel when I got down there, and I followed her—I did not take her home; I left her, and joined Mr. Bridgman—I do not know whether she saw me join Mr. Bridgratn or not, because I came out behind her—I was not with her in the chapel—I went into another pew where they put me—I did not know where to find her—I was never there before—I went to the chapel to hear a sermon—I knew my brother's wife was at the chapel, but not where to find her—the chapel was only three or four doors from where I was lodging—my attention was not called to this case for a week after I came back—I came back on the Monday after the 6th—I have not been asked about being in Ramsgate on the 6th of Sept. till I came here—I might have made a statement in consequence of inquiries about the 6th of Sept., but I do not know when, and I do not know whether I have been asked about it by anybody—I offered to come forward for Mr. Bridgman when I heard of it—I went and told Mr. Bridgman that I was down there on that very same day—that might have been a fortnight or three weeks ago, since there has been an inquiry at Union-hall—I went and asked Mr. Bridgman whether he could recollect that he was down there with me, and if he could recollect the day of the month—he said he could not exactly go into the particulars—I told him it was the 6th, and I understood, from inquiries at Union-hall, that it was the 6th it was committed, and I said, "I was with you on the 6th till nearly nine o'clock, and I will come forward and state it anywhere"—I did not attend at Union-hall at first—the last time I was there—I have never been examined by anybody as to the evidence I was to give, and I never would be—I went to Union-hall to hear the case, and if Mr. Bridgman, as I expected he would do, wanted evidence to prove he was away from home, I intended giving it—I never spoke to any solicitor, or anybody else—I never was at Ramsgate but once before, and I think that is about ten years ago—I went this time to see my brother's family—my brother is a coach-builder, in Morton-street, Cavendish-square—before I was a miller I was a farmer, which I left twenty years ago—I am a housekeeper—I have known Mr. Bridgman from ten to fourteen years.
COURT. Q. What time did you leave London to go out of town on that day? A. I think eleven o'clock—I went from London-bridge—I got to Margate somewhere about five—I staid there about ten minutes or a quarter
of an hour, I cannot say to a few miutes, it was a very short time—I then went on to Ramsgate, to my brother's family—I got there, I think, about seven—I found my brother's wife and one daughter there—my brother was in London—I staid in the house but a very short period before I went to chapel, only a few minutes—I did not go to chapel with my brother's wife, I went after them—my brother's wife was not in the house when I got there—when I said just now that she was there, I meant that she was at Ramsgate—she had gone to chapel—directly I got into the chapel the pew-opener opened a pew and put me in—after leaving Mr. Bridgman, I returned to my brother's, and slept there—it took me about five minutes to get there after leaving Mr. Bridgman—I did not know my way—I inquired—I did not see anything of my brother's wife at the chapel—I was told they were there—I went off from the door with Mr. Bridgman—I dined as I went down that day—I had what I may call tea at a house in Margate, at least a cup of coffee, and a piece of bread and butter, just before I started—I supped at my brother's—I think we had a part of a loin of mutton—my brother's wife, daughter, and I sat down to supper—there was only one daughter then—part of the family had come home the week previous—I left about nine o'clock next morning for Margate—I had no friends at Margate, but I was to meet persons named Brown and Gilham there, whom I had agreed to go down with, but circumstances prevented their going on Tuesday, and I went down before them—I found them on the Thursday—I staid there till the next day, in Brown's company, and we came up together on Monday—I know it was on the 6th that I saw Mr. Bridgman, because I was to have gone down on the Tuesday, but the party I was goiog with could not go that day—I said I would go on the following day, if they would send a note to my house, and they did so—Mr. Brown was at my house on the Monday evening—I did not see Mr. Bridgman coming out of the chapel—I talked to him as if he had been one of the hearers, and I was asking the view he took of the sermon—he told me he had been in the chapel, and I am quite sure he had, from the conversation I had with him—I think service began about seven o'clock—it had begun when I arrived—it lasted till a quarter to nine, as near as I can recollect, I cannot tell to a few minutes, I had no watch, but I got back about nine, or a few minutes past—my sister said, "How came you out so late, do you know what time it is?"—I said, "Yes, somewhere about nine," and she said, "It is five minutes past nine."
MR. BODKIN. Q. Did she see Mr. Bridgman? A. No, neither did she know him.
THOMAS CHAPMAN . I reside in Burton-crescent—I am in no business or profession—I keep the house, and have lived there about eleven years. I was at Ramsgate in the early part of Sept. last—I am acquainted with Mr. Bridgman—I saw him at Ramsgate on the 5th, 6th, and 7th of Sept.—I met him on the pier, and had conversation with him—he dined with a friend of mine on the 6th, and I was with him again on the 7th—it was Wednesday the 6th, Thursday the 7th, and Friday the 8th—it was my error saying the 5th, 6th, and 7th—it was the 6th, 7th, and 8th—Wednesday was the first day I saw him—he dined with my friend on the Thursday, and I saw him again on the Friday, and took leave of him—( looking at a letter)—this is a letter which I wrote on the subject on the 25th of Oct.—I have known Mr. Bridgman about twelve years as a most respectable character—I have not been on perfect intimacy with him, but, as a minister of the truth, I always esteemed him, and have always heard of his most excellent character.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you never in any business? A. Yes, the proprietor of the "Globe" newspaper, and am so still—it was at Mr. Thompson's house that Mr. Bridgman dined on the Thursday—I have nothing particular
which enables me to fix on the 6th as the day I first saw Mr. Bridgman, but I had a friend coming down, and I was waiting for him at the packet—I did not see Mr. Bridgman arrive—I met him on the pier about three or four o'clock in the afternoon—I did not go with him from the pier—we walked a little on the pier, and I had some conversation of a particular nature with him—I am quite sure I cannot be mistaken in the day—I believe I went to Ramsgate about the 10th of Aug.—I made an entry in my pocket-book, of expecting my friend down on the 6th of Sept., as I expected him to dinner—I know it was on the 6th I met Mr. Bridgman on the pier, because I was waiting for my friend, whom I expected that day.
COURT. Q. He did not come? A. No—I was not certain of his coming on that day, but I thought he might—when I saw Mr. Bridgman on the Friday, he was going off by the packet, as I understood, for Dover—I saw him on the pier, and conversed with him—I walked daily on the pier, expecting my friend—I do not know that I had been on the pier on the 5th—I did not always go there—my friend never came—I generally walked on the pier every day for recreation—I made no memorandum of seeing Mr. Bridgman, but the circumstance of expecting my friend riveted it more on my mind, because I entered that in my book—my knowledge of Mr. Bridgman is very casual—I have applied to him more than once, I believe, to preach for a charity, or something of that kind—I never visited at his house, only to call on him to beg him to preach—I cannot say whether he had servants to open the door—I think a servant opened the door—on one occasion I invited him to dine with me, when he was to preach—that is the only occasion I have been in private with him—I have often heard him preach—I cannot say whether he keeps servants or not.
EDWIN EVANS LEECH . I live at No. 7, Upper Sussex-place, Old Kent-road, and am in the Stamp and Tax Office. In the early part of Sept. I was at Ramsgate, and, on Thursday, the 7th of Sept., saw Mr. Bridgman there about nine o'clock in the evening—I also saw him on the following day.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Where did you see him on the 7th? A. On the pier—I had been a fortnight at Ramsgate for relaxation—I do not know Mr. Chapman or Mr. Redding—I have known Mr. Bridgman about ten years or more—I became acquainted with him through friends and relatives of mine, my father for instance—he is not in any business—he is a housekeeper, and so am I—I know it was Thursday, the 7th, that I saw him, because I invited him to dine with me, and he regretted that a previous invitation would not allow him to do so—I then wished him to name the subsequent day, Friday—he said he could not, for he was going to start in the boat that afternoon for Dover—I was lodging at Ramsgate with my family—I have occasionally attended Mr. Bridgman's chapel at Walworth, but not regularly—I am not related to him—I do not recollect whether I was on the pier on the Wednesday afternoon when the Ramsgate packet arrived—I was on the Thursday—I was at Cavendish Chapel on the Wednesday night—I did not see Mr. Bridgman there—I went there to hear a popular preacher, a Mr. Daniels—I do not recollect the text—it is a Dissenting chapel—I do not know how often they have service in the week—I do not know what time service begins in the week—I went about a quarter past seven o'clock—I do not know what time I came out—it might be a little after eight—I did not observe—I was there somewhere about an hour, I think—I do not think it could have been two hours—I then went home to where I was staying, at Belle Vue, on the eastern cliff—I took tea at home that night about ten—I got home about a quarter to ten—I went circuitously home from the chapel, by the sea side, round the sands—it took me about an hour to get home—to the best of my recollection there was a clock in the chapel—service was over when I left—I do not know whether it
was as late as a quarter to nine when I left the chapel—I said eight because I went about a quarter past seven, and was there, I thought, about an hour, or it might be a little more, I really cannot say—I cannot tell whether it was a long sermon—I think I had been there about twice during my stay at Ramsgate—I took a note of seeing Mr. Bridgman on the 7th.
Q. How soon were you applied to about this after you came to town? A. I was applied to by a letter, which appeared to have miscarried, about a week ago—I do not know the contents of it—I came forward spontaneously, on seeing the accounts in the papers—I was not before the Magistrate.
COURT. Q. Were you on the pier on Thursday, when the packet arrived? A. Yes—there were two arrived, one about four o'clock, and the other about five—I do not know whether any packets arrived before—I do not know at what time all the packets arrive.
CHARLES TOWNLEY . I reside in West-street, Camberwell—I hold, an appointment in the Trinity-house, and have done so thirty-seven years—I have known Mr. Bridgman twelve or thirteen years, ever since he has been there—I was at Ramsgate previous to Sept., and remained there up to the 14th or 15th of Sept.—I met Mr. Bridgman there on Wednesday morning, the 6th of Sept., about a quarter or twenty minutes past nine, in Ramsgate harbour—I spoke to him, and was in company with him on and off for an hour and a half, witnessing the landing of some troops and horses from a transport—I saw him again, about a quarter past five that afternoon, coming from Camdensquare, between there and High-street, on my way to Broad-street—I merely passed the compliments of the day to him then, as a lady was with him—I saw him again either on Thursday or Friday morning, but I cannot say which, at Ramsgate.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What enables you to fix on Wednesday the 6th of Sept.? A. I am satisfied it was that day, because that very morning the China transport came into the harbour with troops, and a number of persons went down to see them disembark—I went to see it, and met Mr. Bridgman there—it is stated in Lloyd's list, that the China arrived on the 6th, and a part of my family went to Dover that day, and in their absence I went over to Broadstairs—on my way there, met Mr. Bridgman coming from Camdensquare—I have Lloyd's list in my hand, which states that the China arrived at Ramsgate on the 6th—I am sure the vessel I saw was the China —I saw her name—I have very seldom been to Mr. Bridgman's chapel—I have lived in West-street twenty-six or twenty-seven years—I was at Ramsgate three weeks—( referring to a memorandum)—I went on the 26th of August—I have no memorandum of meeting Mr. Bridgman on the 6th of Sept.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What made you go to Broadstairs? A. I had engaged with a part of my family that went to Dover on the Wednesday, that we would go to Broadstairs in the afternoon, and meet them on their return—I am quite satisfied that it was on that very same day I saw Mr. Bridgman—the China had arrived from Quebec.
COURT. Q. Did you meet Mr. Bridgman on the pier? A. Yes; I did not see him land—he was walking at the end of the pier to view the troops disembarking—the usual hour of the packets arriving from London, was three or four, and sometimes five, it depended on the wind and tide—two came every day.
CHARLES TOWNLEY JUN . I am a commercial clerk, and live in Camberwell-grove. I was at Ramsgate early in Sept., and saw Mr. Bridgman there on Wednesday the 6th, and Thursday the 7th—I had no conversation with him.
MR. CLARKON. Q. Were you acquainted with him at all? A. I have known him for the last ten years, I think ever since he has been at West-street chapel, and should know him when I met with him—I went to Dover on the 6th of Sept. about half-past nine in the morning, and saw him on Ramsgate pier about that time—I returned the same afternoon—I do not know when Mr. Bridgman was staying—I was not applied to to become a witness, I came forward voluntarily about a fortnight ago.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Was that from observing in the newspapers a report of what was taking place before the Magistrate? A. Yes—I was not applied to by Mr. Bridgman—I more particularly remember seeing him on Thursday morning, about twelve o'clock, on the sands—I was going towards Broadstairs—I have not the slightest doubt about it—I was only there one week—I went down on Saturday, the 2nd of Sept., and returned on the 9th—I only went to Dover that one Wednesday, and it was as I was starting that I saw him on the pier, and next morning my daughter and younger sister were riding on donkeys on the sands, towards Broadstairs, and they almost ran over Mr. Bridgman.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is your daughter here? A. No—she is only seven years of age.
JOHN MASON THOMPSON . I live at No. 9, Chancery-lane. I was at Ramsgate in the early part of Sept., and saw Mr. Bridgman, whom I knew, there on Thursday the 7th, as near as I can recollect, between twelve and one o'clock—a particular circumstance enables me to speak accurately to the day—not knowing how long he might continue at Ramsgate, I asked him to fix a time to dine with me—he named the next day, but as I was engaged on the next day to dine with some friends, I told him I was sorry I could not receive him then, but should be glad if he would dine with me that day, and after some little hesitation, he said, "Well, I did not promise to return to my friends, and therefore I will do so," and accordingly he dined with me on Thursday the 7th—but for that circumstance I certainly should not have recollected the day.
COURT. Q. Why, did he not tell you he was going to Dover on Friday? A. I think he said he was going to Dover in the afternoon of the next day, Friday—I am not exactly certain with respect to the conversation about Dover—I knew he was going to Dover, but whether it was on the Friday or Saturday, I cannot say.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Are you able to state, on your oath, that he dined with you on Thursday the 7th of Sept.? A. Certainly, I have not the slightest doubt about it—being an invalid, I dined at two o'clock, and that I told him—it was rather later that day, in consequence of there being some little addition made—I am sure I gave him the invitation the morning I met him—I have had a casual acquaintance with him for, perhaps, two or three years, but I never spoke to him above three or four times—he once dined with me at my own house, in consequence of his preaching where I attend—knowing he was to preach in the evening, I asked him to dine with me—I never was in his chapel.
JOSEPH BARBER WILKS . I am cashier to the East India Company, and live at No. 1, Pleasant-place, Camberwell. In the early part of Sept., I was stopping with my family at Ramsgate—I saw Mr. Bridgman there on the 5th of Sept.—I went on the pier with my family, and met Mr. Bridgman unexpectedly, just arrived by the boat—I it was about five, but I cannot state—I did not see him land, but met him on the pier among the people, who then landed from the boat, which came from London—I went
up and shook hands with him, having known him some years, and having a very great respect for him, I said, "Mr. Bridgman, I see you are alone, come home with me," and he accompanied me home with my family—I was living at No. 10, Camden-place—he took tea with us on the 5th, on his arrival, and took supper the same evening—I invited him to make my house his home, consequently he breakfasted on the following morning (Wednesday) dined, took tea, and supped on Wednesday, leaving my house from half past ten till eleven—he breakfasted on Thursday—he did not dine, but took tea, and supped on Thursday, breakfasted and dined on Friday, and on Friday afternoon I and my family accompanied him to the pier, and saw him on board the Dover packet about six o'clock.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. What time did you dine on Wednesday? A. We generally dined about two o'clock—we took tea about six—I think he did not stay at home the rest of the evening—he merely took his meals with me—he told me at supper that he had been to chapel—he went out after tea, and returned in the evening, I cannot say at what time, perhaps about nine—he told me the text, and the particulars.
COURT. Q. Do you remember the text? A. Yes, it was "Fear not to go down into Egypt, I will go with thee"—or something of that sort, relating to Joseph and his brethren, I think, but I am not quite clear at this moment—he told me the text at the time, and it was rather a remarkable one.
MR. BODKIN. Q. How long have you known him? A. Ten or twelve years, and always considered him a most excellent man.
COURT. Q. Did you ever visit at his house? A. Yes—I have called, and have seen one female servant there—the younger prisoner is his only grown up son—there are three children altogether—-the others are quite children, not old enough to be witnesses—I know the days on which I saw Mr. Bridgman to be the 5th, 6th, and 7th, because I went down on the 23rd of Aug., and had been there a fortnight all but a day—my eldest son was to have come down to spend the last week—I intended remaining four weeks, but the weather being exceedingly fine I wrote to him at the end of the second week to come down a week earlier, and he came down on the 5th, the day Mr. Bridgman did—he is not here—I have a letter here which he wrote on the Monday, saying that he should come down the following day if fine—here is the post-mark of 4th Sept. on it—on the front is the Walworth post-mark, and on the other part is the Ramsgate post-mark of the 5th Sept.—he says on the outside, "I intend coming down to-morrow"—he dined and drank tea with Mr. Bridgman as well as myself.
EDWARD MASON THOMPSON . I am the son of Mr. Thompson, who has been examined—I was with him at Ramsgate in Sept.—I know Mr. Bridgman, and saw him at Ramsgate on Thursday the 7th of Sept., between one and two o'clock, on the pier with my father.
COURT to GEORGE GARFORD. Q. You state that this occurred on Wednesday the 6th, what fixes the day in your mind? A. I can scarcely say what can bring it to my remembrance now—I know I was with Mr. Tawney on the following Monday.
JOHN COLSON . I am an officer of Guy's Hospital. The younger defendant has been a student of surgery there for some time, and had the character of an assiduous, zealous student.
J. W. BRIDGMAN — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Twelve Months.
ISAAC BRIDGMAN — NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

3105. RICHARD NORBURY was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of Sept., 2 metal cocks, value 6 d.; 1 flannel, 1 d.; and 1 bell-crank, 1 d.; the goods of George Giles Weston Thatcher; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES FAULKNER . I am a whitesmith, in the employ of George Giles Weston Thatcher, an ironmonger and brazier, at Clapham. On the morning of the 20th of Sept., about half-past eleven o'clock, the prisoner came to the workshop—he did not ask for anything—I saw him take the tap off the board, and put it into his pocket—I did not see him pick the brass crank up—he sat there a little while—I went in to see if my master was in the front shop, and while I was gone he walked out—this is my master's property.
Prisoner. Q. Did you see me take it off the bench? A. Yes—I can swear to this tap—I took it out of the boiler myself, and put a new one on—I saw you take it off the board on the window ledge, and put it into your pocket—you did not take it from one pocket and put it into another.
ROBERT MOORE ( police-constable V 12.) In consequence of information, I took the prisoner into custody, searched him, and found on him these taps, this bell-crank, and several small pieces of iron, which are here.
GEORGE GILES WESTON THATCHER . I am an ironmonger, at Clapham. I have seen this tap about the premises, on the bench—I cannot swear to it—there is always a good deal of metal lying about—the prisoner never worked for me—I have known him at work at different shops in Clapham for these two years.
Prisoner's Defence. It is my own property.
SAMUEL EASOM ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from the clerk of the peace for Surrey—( read)—I was present at the trial—he is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3106]
3106. WILLIAM ROBINSON was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of Oct., 1 cask, value 3 s.; and 445 lbs. weight of white lead, 5 l.; the goods of John Spencer Chadwell; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN SPENCER CHADWELL . I am an oil and colourman, and live in Great Suffolk-street, Southwark. I have seen a cask of white lead in the prisoner's possession, which is my property—I know it by the weight and tare marks on the cask—I had such in my shop on the 23rd of Oct., at six o'clock in the afternoon, and missed it about half-past six or seven next morning—I noticed some marks, which appeared as if some cask had been rolled away—I traced it up the court, at the back of my premises, then went into my shop, and sent for a policeman—I showed him the marks, and fancied he would find my lead by them.
Prisoner. Q. Where did the cask lay? A. At the side of my door, in the road—I have had occasion to complain of boys rolling my casks in the street.
GEORGE WILD ( police-constable M 94.) In consequence of information, I went to the court on the following morning, the 24th—I traced the cask directly to the prisoner's door—there are three houses in the court—I knocked, and he looked out of the window—he was then in bed—I knew he lived in that house, but did not know who he was—I asked him to come down and let me in—he did so—he opened the door, and said, "I suppose you are comefor the tub I fell over last night, about half-past eleven or twelve o'clock"—I
said, "I have not said anything at all about any cask"—he took me directlyto the stairs, and in a cupboard was the white lead in the cask—I sent for assistance—the prosecutor and his apprentice came, and we got it out—I took it, and took the prisoner into custody.
JANE CHADWELL . I am the prosecutor's wife. On the night in question I saw the prisoner from ten till eleven o'clock—I mentioned the loss of the cask to him—he condoled with my loss, and said no doubt it was sold before it was stolen.
Prisoner's Defence. I fell over the cask in the court, got a light, saw no direction who it belonged to, and took it into my house. When the policeman came, I said, "I suppose you have come for the cask I stumbled over last night, coming home."
JOHN CLEMENTS . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person tried and convicted.
GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18431023-3107]
3107. JAMES HAINES and EDWARD BYFIELD was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of August, 1 rule, value 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of William Cooper, their master.
MESSRS. PAYNE and CARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM COOPER . I am a builder, and live at pulse-bill—the prisoners were in my service, about February last, as plasterers—I believe they were working in Clapham New-park—I had a rule there—I was there almost every day—I saw that rule there afterwards, and it had been altered—it had been made narrower at each end, which was for some purposes more convenient than if it was a regular size all the way through, as I had had it made—this is it—( looking at it)—it had been as wide at the ends as it is in the middle—I had it made about twelve months ago—I observed "E B "was cut on it, which had not been on it before—I saw Byfield when be came to the building—I asked how he dared to take and put his name on my rule—he said it was not my rule, that a person named Wren made it for him, for doing a job just above—I said, every man on the building knew it was my rule, by a singular place on it, that Wren never made it, and every person in the building knew it to be the truth—he then said if Wren did not make it for him, it belonged to Haines—I said, if he dared to take it away, or Haines either, I should lock them up wherever I found them, for stealing the rule, as it was mine—this was in the latter end of May—I was at the building, and observed the rule was missing in July, and I sent to the prisoners, by a boy who they employed to work for them—I sent three different messages—Haines at last came to me on the 7th of August, and brought this rule, it was on a Monday morning, and he seemed as if he had been drinking—he said, "Do you call this rule yours?"—I said, "Yes, certainly I do call it mine"—he said, "You are a d—liar"—he said, "I will tell you how I came by that rule; it belonged to the Asphalte Company "—I said it certainly did not—he said, "Their name is on the rule now"—I said it certainly was not, "E. Byfield" was on it—he said it was not—I turned it up, and said, "There it is"—he said "He had a right to put it on, he and I are partners"—I said, "Where is the Asphalte Company's mark?"—he said if it was properly cleaned it would appear—I said it was not, he persisted in taking it away, and pretended as if he was going to knock me down—I said, "If that is it, I shall not leave you till I give you into custody"—I followed him about a mile, and then gave him into custody—I
saw Byfield some distance off, and game him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Who it your attorney? A. Iam my own—in the first instance I employed Mr. Cripps, an attorney——he said, "I will help you get the bill"—I cannot exactly say when I first missed the rule from the premises—it was about the latter end of May I saw the mark on it—it was on the premises while the prisoners were working for me-—Haines had worked for me twenty-five years, and they had not left off finally after the rule was gone—I think it might be about two months afterwards that I ceased to employ them, because they had got this piece-work, and they defied me to keep them out of the premises—I had no quarrel with them about leaving my work to go on with other, no further than telling them I should set on other hands—I am sorry to say By field lived rather too near roe—I should not think of prosecuting for this rule, only knowing I was repeatedly robbed before—Byfield lived not above 200 or 300 yards from this building, in Mill-lane, I believe—the house has no number—Haines has lived about Brixton twenty-fire years—Haines called upon me on the 7th of Aug.—I had repeatedly claimed the rule of them before that—I sent three distinct messages that they were not to deceive themselves, provided they did not bring that rule back to the place, I should have them locked up—when I first saw the name on it I claimed it—I did not claim it twenty times—I saw By field, and said to him, "Do not deceive yourself, I shall certainly have you locked up"—he did not say I was a liar—he said he bad nothing to do with it, Haines had got it—I went before a Magistrate, and he said, "I should like to do away with regard to the felony, this might be a mistake of these men"—I cannot say whether any deposition was taken, I think there was—they only heard one witness that I had—the prisoners called two witnesses to prove the rule was not mine—I had three witnesses in Court, bot they only heard one—I did not wish to drive the case for the Magistrate to send it to trial—I wished him to give them reprimand—he did not discharge them—he laid, "I do not discharge them, I leave it to you to indict them at the Sessions."
Q. Did they immediately afterwards bring an action against you for false imprisonment? A. Yes, but that was only to bounce it over me, that I should not go to the Sessions—the Magistrate said, "You can take the rule, and do as you like"—I said, "No, I won't, they may contend that I altered the rule," and the rule has been in the hands of the policeman ever since—the prisoners served me with a process—I dare say that was dose to get rid of me—the solicitor came and tried to laugh me out of it.
Q. Did not Haines come to you on the 7th or 8th of August, and say, "Here is the rule, do you mean to say it is yours?" and did not you call him a thief? A. Certainly not—I never said such a word—it was to the same purpose—I do not recollect that he said I should hear of it again—Haines took away the rule in August—I followed him about a mile, then met a policeman, and gave him into custody—he did not ask the policeman permission to go and change his clothes, and to call on By field, bis partner—I saw By field about twenty yards off—I do not know whether he was at work or no, but he came to the gate, and I said, "Policeman, this man has been guilty of the same act as the other, I charge him as well"—I did not say the rule had been made two years ago—I said I knew it by its having a flaw—this is the flaw—( looking at it)—I employed an attorney to find the bill—I do not know that the prisoner! had been told by my attorney that he would take no further steps till they came to surrender—it is no such thing—Mr. Cripps told me he had seen their attorney, and, for honour sake, he would not take them without giving them notice—I waited here all day, and did not say anything further to Mr. Cripps; and, after
standing about here till Tuesday, I said, "I won't stand about here in this way"—I went to Union-ball, and applied for the warrant—I believe that was about four o'clock—the policeman went with me, and the prisoners were locked up that night, I believe, and taken the next day before the Magistrate—he said he had no power—he certainly did not let the prisoners go, because they came to Newgate, and laid here all nights—I appeared before Mr. Justice Erskine last Sessions—he ordered the prisoners to be admitted to bail—on that J asked for my expenses, and he said I should have every farthing—he said I should have them, if this did not turn out to be a vindictive prosecution—I certainly never said, that if they would abandon their action I would my prosecution, or any thing of the kind.
MR. CARTISN. Q. Was it the first time you saw the letters on the rule that you spoke to Byfield about it? A. Two or three daya after, the first time I got sight of him—the indictment was found on the Monday in the last Sessions, about twelve or one o'clock—I applied for the warrant on Tuesday, and it was put into my hand about four o'clock—it was executed that night, I believe.
JURY. Q. Did you find the rest of their tools? A. Yes, but I would almost as lief decline answering anything—I bad missed property, and had accused them—we generally find tools, but in this case I was to find every thing, except wax for casting, and the other tools carried in a basket.
DAVID REEVE . I live at Brixton. I have worked for Mr. Cooper—I made this rule, and sent it to the building—I know it—( looking at it)—it is just as it was when I made it—I bad some words about it with Mr. Cooper—I said it would never be fit for a floating-rale—I said it was a very shaky piece, not proper for the purpose—I am sure this is the rule—it is not exactly in the same state, it is a little narrowed at each end—it was straight all along when I gave it to the boy to take down—I made it about twelve months ago last April.
Cross-examined. Q. What is it worth? A. Not a great deal, something under 10 s.; I suppose it is worth about 2 s. 6 d. or 3 s.—what I know it by is this flaw, which it shakes with—I described it as not fit for the purpose—I am quite sure I did not swear that I made it three yean ago—I did not all the Magistrate it was three years—I do not know that I said it was two years—it was about twelve months last April.
JURY. Q. Do you know it by anything but the shake? A. No, sir—I can swear to the wood, as being the wood I prepared.
JOHN MILLER . I am a bricklayer. I worked for Mr. Cooper last May—I worked for a short time at the building where the prisoners were at work—I remember, about May, Byfield came there, and Mr. Cooper accused him of stealing the rule—he asked him why he put bis name on the rule—Byfield said it was his—Mr. Cooper said it was not, it was a rule belonging to himself—I do not recollect anything else.
PETER CHARLES . I am a bricklayer. I was at work at Mr. Cooper's buildings in May. I remember Mr. Cooper asking Byfield what business he had to put his name on his rule—Byfield said it was his rule; and he said as far as this, that it was bis intention to take the rule away—Mr. Cooper said, "It is mine, and you may depend, if you take that rule away, I will lock you up"—Byfield said he would insist on taking it George Brydges (police-constable P 128.) I was in Coalharbour-lane on the 7th of August—I saw Haines with this rule, and I took him into custody by Mr. Cooper's direction—I afterwards saw Byfield, and took him—I took both the prisoners to the station—I was at the police-office afterwards—the Magistrate said Mr. Cooper might take his rule.
GEORGE SPIGGET . I am a carpenter. I have been at work for Mr. Cooper—I know this rule—I first saw it last April twelvemonth—I was working for a plasterer then, and fetched the rule for the plasterer from David Reeve's—I have seen it once since—I am able to swear positively to it—it is the same rule.
Cross-examined. Q. You know it by the flaw, I suppose? A. Yes.
HENRY COOPER . I am the prosecutor's son. I remember, on the 7th of August, Haines came to my father's house, and brought this rule—he asked my father whether he meant to say that that rule was his—my father said he should know it amongst a thousand—Haines said it was his, and he would tell him where he got it; when he was at work at a house in Streatham Paragon, the rule was there, and it belonged to the Asphalte Company, and they left it at the house, and the people of the house gave it up to him; and he said, if it was cleaned, the Asphalte Company's name was on it then, and he could show it him in a minute—he then left with the rule.
MR. CLARKSON called
WILLIAM ALLEN . I am a bricklayer. I know the two prisoners—I knew Byfield while at work at Brixton-hill, two years ago last March—while he was going off the scaffold I saw his floating-rule fall—he was in the act of shoving the tools on one side of the scaffold—the rule slipped off the scaffold, and I saw this nail driven in it on that occasion—this is the rule—( looking at it)—I say so on account of this mark—it fell on the ground, and this end of it fell against a oil), and made this mark on it—I saw the rule split, and I law him drive this nail into it—I told him the rule was not injured a bit—I could swear to it among ten thousand—I was examined before the Magistrate—I saw the rule chip by the "E. B.," and I saw Byfield drive the nail into it; and seeing it now with this indenture, and this nail, I have not the least doubt it is the same—I have known Byfield four years—his character has been very honourable—I never knew him charged with anything.
MR. CARTEEN. Q. By what do you take on yourself to swear to this rule? A. I have explained it by this being chipped off, touching the "E. B." which is on it—he said when it fell, "D—you, this is all the good you hare done me"—I said, "It has not hurt your rule nor you, but very near knocked your name out"—this was in the middle of last March two years—I had never seen the rule before, to my knowledge—I have seen it since, at Union-hall—I tee it now, and I have seen it at Brixton—I do not know that I can say candidly that I have seen it any other time—I never examined it before the day the accident happened—I examined it at Union-hall, and I hare examined it now—this accident happened to it at Brixton-bill, at Mr. Muggeridge's house.
JURY. Q. Did you drive the nail? A. No—Byfield did, and I believe can tell you what the nail is—about two years elapsed between my first seeing the rule and my seeing it again—Byfield called on me, and said, "It is the same rule that you saw fall from the scaffold/' and I remembered that I said, "It has not hurt you, but nearly knocked your name off."
JAMES WAY . I served my time with Mr. Haines. He has lived in Brixton twenty-five years, and was an honest, upright man as ever lived—I never heard him charged with any offence.
MR. CARTEEN. Q. Have you ever worked for Mr. Cooper? A. Yet, I have, when Haines has been there—I never heard Mr. Cooper accuse him of anything—I have brought this rule from Haines's place to Mr. Cooper's home.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When Haines was at work, and Cooper saw it there? A. Yes—he saw me use it there—it was the latter end of April, or the beginning of May that I commenced there, I cannot say which—Haines was there.
NOT GUILTY
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

JAMES CLARKE . I am a painter. I left Mr. Duckie's premises on the 6th of Oct.—I returned in ten minutes, and found these two prisoners had opened the coach-house door, and got these brushes and coat—I found these three dusting brushes under Western's handkerchief—they are Henry Hatfield's—I had left this coat of mine in the back stable—it waa removed from thence to the coach-house, where I found the prisoners.
